If, however, two rows in $A$ or in $B$ are interchanged, then the $c_i^{(h)}$ change because the factors in the individual products of the sum are grouped differently; but if corresponding interchanges are made simultaneously in the rows of $A$ and of $B$, the $c_i^{(h)}$ remain unchanged, since only the individual terms of the sum are thereby interchanged.

By turning the rows into columns in $A$ or in $B$, or in both at once, one obtains three further different ways of forming the product of two determinants in determinant form. This can also be expressed as follows. To form the product of two determinants, one may multiply the elements of the separate rows or columns of one factor by the corresponding elements of the rows or columns of the other factor, add the products, and regard these sums of products as the elements of a new determinant.

The multiplication rule can be applied to a product of two determinants with different numbers of elements by transforming the determinant with the smaller number of rows, by the theorem § 22, IX, into another with more rows.

\section*{§ 28. Determinants of subdeterminants.}

We immediately make an application here of the multiplication law of determinants. Let, as before,
\[
A=\begin{vmatrix}
 a_1^{(1)}&a_2^{(1)}&\cdots&a_n^{(1)}\\
 a_1^{(2)}&a_2^{(2)}&\cdots&a_n^{(2)}\\
 \cdots&\cdots&&\cdots\\
 a_1^{(n)}&a_2^{(n)}&\cdots&a_n^{(n)}
\end{vmatrix},\tag{1}
\]
and let
\[
\begin{matrix}
 A_1^{(1)}&A_2^{(1)}&\cdots&A_n^{(1)}\\
 A_1^{(2)}&A_2^{(2)}&\cdots&A_n^{(2)}\\
 \cdots&\cdots&&\cdots\\
 A_1^{(n)}&A_2^{(n)}&\cdots&A_n^{(n)}
\end{matrix}\tag{2}
\]
be the system of subdeterminants. If we form from (2) the determinant which we shall denote by $\Delta$, we may apply the multiplication rule to the product $A\Delta$. By § 22, (3) and (7), this gives
\[
 A\Delta=\begin{vmatrix}
 A&0&\cdots&0\\
 0&A&\cdots&0\\
 \cdots&\cdots&&\cdots\\
 0&0&\cdots&A
\end{vmatrix}=A^n,
\]
and, by division by $A$,
\begin{equation}
 \Delta=A^{n-1}.\tag{3}
\end{equation}
Thus $\Delta$ is the $(n-1)$st power of $A$. In this derivation it has indeed first been assumed that $A$ is different from zero. But since (3) is an identity with respect to the elements $a_i^{(h)}$, that is, since it also holds when these quantities are independent variables, it follows that the formula (3) still holds in the exceptional case; that is, if $A$ vanishes, then $\Delta$ also vanishes.

This result is a special case of a more general theorem by which any determinant of the matrix (2) may be formed. Consider the $\nu$-rowed subdeterminant
\[
\Delta_\nu=\begin{vmatrix}
 A_1^{(1)}&A_2^{(1)}&\cdots&A_\nu^{(1)}\\
 A_1^{(2)}&A_2^{(2)}&\cdots&A_\nu^{(2)}\\
 \cdots&\cdots&&\cdots\\
 A_1^{(\nu)}&A_2^{(\nu)}&\cdots&A_\nu^{(\nu)}
\end{vmatrix},\tag{4}
\]
from which all other $\nu$-rowed subdeterminants can be derived by permutation of the upper and lower indices. One may apply the multiplication rule by transforming $\Delta_\nu$, according to the concluding remark of the last paragraph, into an $n$-rowed determinant.

Put
\[
\Delta_\nu=\begin{vmatrix}
 A_1^{(1)}&\cdots&A_\nu^{(1)}&A_{\nu+1}^{(1)}&\cdots&A_n^{(1)}\\
 \cdots&&\cdots&\cdots&&\cdots\\
 A_1^{(\nu)}&\cdots&A_\nu^{(\nu)}&A_{\nu+1}^{(\nu)}&\cdots&A_n^{(\nu)}\\
 0&\cdots&0&1&\cdots&0\\
 \cdots&&\cdots&\cdots&&\cdots\\
 0&\cdots&0&0&\cdots&1
\end{vmatrix},\tag{5}
\]
where $n-\nu$ rows and columns have been adjoined, the former containing only zeros except in the diagonal terms. If one now forms the product $A\Delta_\nu$, it follows that
\[
 A\Delta_\nu=A^\nu
 \begin{vmatrix}
 a_{\nu+1}^{(\nu+1)}&\cdots&a_n^{(\nu+1)}\\
 \cdots&&\cdots\\
 a_{\nu+1}^{(n)}&\cdots&a_n^{(n)}
 \end{vmatrix},\tag{6}
\]
and this is by Theorem IX, § 22. Dividing here by $A$ and applying the notation of § 23, one obtains
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_\nu=A^{\nu-1}A_{1,2,\ldots,\nu}^{1,2,\ldots,\nu}.\tag{7}
\end{equation}
For $\nu=2$ this gives the special result
\begin{equation}
 A_1^{(1)}A_2^{(2)}-A_1^{(2)}A_2^{(1)}=A A_{1,2}^{1,2}.\tag{8}
\end{equation}
We shall use formula (8) often later. In the notation by differential quotients it can be represented as
\begin{equation}
 A\frac{\partial^2A}{\partial a_1^{(1)}\partial a_2^{(2)}}
 =\frac{\partial A}{\partial a_1^{(1)}}\frac{\partial A}{\partial a_2^{(2)}}
 -\frac{\partial A}{\partial a_1^{(2)}}\frac{\partial A}{\partial a_2^{(1)}}.\tag{9}
\end{equation}
It is especially important in the case where $A$ is a symmetric determinant, so that $a_i^{(k)}=a_k^{(i)}$; then also
\[
 \frac{\partial A}{\partial a_1^{(2)}}=\frac{\partial A}{\partial a_2^{(1)}},
\]
where in the differentiation no account is taken of the dependence $a_i^{(k)}=a_k^{(i)}$. Then formula (9) becomes
\begin{equation}
 A\frac{\partial^2A}{\partial a_1^{(1)}\partial a_2^{(2)}}
 =\frac{\partial A}{\partial a_1^{(1)}}\frac{\partial A}{\partial a_2^{(2)}}
 -\left(\frac{\partial A}{\partial a_1^{(2)}}\right)^2.\tag{10}
\end{equation}

\section*{§ 29. Interpolation.}

In § 9, under a special assumption, we already solved the problem of determining an integral rational function which assumes prescribed values for a sufficient number of prescribed values of the argument. We shall now solve this problem generally by applying determinants. First, however, we shall put forward the following observations.

In § 22, (12), the value of the determinant
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
 \alpha_1^{n-1}&\alpha_1^{n-2}&\cdots&\alpha_1&1\\
 \alpha_2^{n-1}&\alpha_2^{n-2}&\cdots&\alpha_2&1\\
 \cdots&\cdots&&\cdots&\cdots\\
 \alpha_n^{n-1}&\alpha_n^{n-2}&\cdots&\alpha_n&1
\end{vmatrix}
\]
was found to be equal to the product of differences
\begin{equation}
(\alpha_1-\alpha_2)(\alpha_1-\alpha_3)\cdots(\alpha_1-\alpha_n)
(\alpha_2-\alpha_3)\cdots(\alpha_2-\alpha_n)\cdots(\alpha_{n-1}-\alpha_n).\tag{1}
\end{equation}
For brevity we now denote this product of differences by
\begin{equation}
 [\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\alpha_3,\ldots,\alpha_n],\tag{2}
\end{equation}
so that the quantity $[\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\alpha_3,\ldots,\alpha_n]$ changes its sign when two of the elements $\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,\alpha_n$ are interchanged.

We further define an integral rational function $f(x)$ of degree $n$ in the variable $x$ by the equation
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=(x-\alpha_1)(x-\alpha_2)\cdots(x-\alpha_n),\tag{3}
\end{equation}
so that, by § 13,
\begin{align}
 f'(\alpha_1)&=(\alpha_1-\alpha_2)(\alpha_1-\alpha_3)\cdots(\alpha_1-\alpha_n),\notag\\
 f'(\alpha_2)&=(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(\alpha_2-\alpha_3)\cdots(\alpha_2-\alpha_n),\tag{4}\\
 &\vdots\notag\\
 f'(\alpha_n)&=(\alpha_n-\alpha_1)(\alpha_n-\alpha_2)\cdots(\alpha_n-\alpha_{n-1}).\notag
\end{align}
In the product of all these expressions each factor of the product (1) occurs twice with opposite sign; since the number of factors of (1) is $\frac{n(n-1)}2$, we obtain the equation
\begin{equation}
 f'(\alpha_1)f'(\alpha_2)\cdots f'(\alpha_n)=(-1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}2}[\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n]^2.\tag{5}
\end{equation}
But with the aid of the relations (4) we may also represent our product (1) as follows:
\begin{align}
 [\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n]
 &= f'(\alpha_1)[\alpha_2,\alpha_3,\ldots,\alpha_n]\notag\\
 &=-f'(\alpha_2)[\alpha_1,\alpha_3,\ldots,\alpha_n]\tag{6}\\
 &=\cdots=(-1)^{n-1}f'(\alpha_n)[\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_{n-1}],\notag
\end{align}
where on the right-hand side each bracketed expression contains one element fewer than the bracketed expression on the left.

Now pose the problem of determining an integral rational function $\varphi(x)$ of degree $(n-1)$ which, for the argument values $x=\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,\alpha_n$, assumes in order the prescribed values $\varphi(\alpha_1),\varphi(\alpha_2),\ldots,\varphi(\alpha_n)$.

If one puts
\begin{equation}
 \varphi(x)=a_1x^{n-1}+a_2x^{n-2}+\cdots+a_{n-1}x+a_n,\tag{7}
\end{equation}
then the $n$ unknown coefficients $a_1,a_2,
\ldots,a_n$ are to be determined from the linear equations
\begin{align}
 \varphi(\alpha_1)&=a_1\alpha_1^{n-1}+a_2\alpha_1^{n-2}+\cdots+a_{n-1}\alpha_1+a_n,\notag\\
 \varphi(\alpha_2)&=a_1\alpha_2^{n-1}+a_2\alpha_2^{n-2}+\cdots+a_{n-1}\alpha_2+a_n,\tag{8}\\
 &\vdots\notag\\
 \varphi(\alpha_n)&=a_1\alpha_n^{n-1}+a_2\alpha_n^{n-2}+\cdots+a_{n-1}\alpha_n+a_n.\notag
\end{align}
The determinant of this system is precisely the quantity (2), and the problem is therefore always soluble by § 26 when this quantity is different from zero, that is, when no two of the $n$ quantities $\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,\alpha_n$ are equal.

Instead of solving the equations (8) by § 26 and substituting the expressions so found into (7), it is preferable, by § 24, II, to eliminate the homogeneous quantities $1,a_1,a_2,
\ldots,a_n$ from the $n+1$ equations (7) and (8). This gives the determinant equation
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
 \varphi(x)&x^{n-1}&x^{n-2}&\cdots&x&1\\
 \varphi(\alpha_1)&\alpha_1^{n-1}&\alpha_1^{n-2}&\cdots&\alpha_1&1\\
 \cdots&\cdots&\cdots&&\cdots&\cdots\\
 \varphi(\alpha_n)&\alpha_n^{n-1}&\alpha_n^{n-2}&\cdots&\alpha_n&1
\end{vmatrix}=0.\tag{9}
\]
We now expand this determinant according to the elements of the first column. For the subdeterminants which occur there we can use the notation (2), and we obtain
\begin{align}
&\varphi(x)[\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n]
-\varphi(\alpha_1)[x,\alpha_2,\alpha_3,\ldots,\alpha_n]
+\varphi(\alpha_2)[x,\alpha_1,\alpha_3,\ldots,\alpha_n]
\notag\\
&\qquad+\cdots+(-1)^n\varphi(\alpha_n)[x,\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_{n-1}]=0.\tag{10}
\end{align}
Now by (1)
\[
 [x,\alpha_2,\alpha_3,
 \ldots,\alpha_n]=(x-\alpha_2)(x-\alpha_3)\cdots(x-\alpha_n)[\alpha_2,\alpha_3,
 \ldots,\alpha_n].
\]
Dividing (10) by $[\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,\alpha_n]$ and taking account of the several expressions (6), one finally obtains
\begin{equation}
 \varphi(x)=f(x)\left\{\frac{\varphi(\alpha_1)}{(x-\alpha_1)f'(\alpha_1)}+\frac{\varphi(\alpha_2)}{(x-\alpha_2)f'(\alpha_2)}+\cdots+\frac{\varphi(\alpha_n)}{(x-\alpha_n)f'(\alpha_n)}\right\}.\tag{11}
\end{equation}
This completely determines the function $\varphi(x)$. This expression for $\varphi(x)$ is called the interpolation formula of Lagrange. That it satisfies the stipulated requirements can be verified afterwards very easily by putting $x=\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,\alpha_n$. The denominators $x-\alpha_1,
\ldots,x-\alpha_n$ are present in it only apparently.




\section*{Third Section. The roots of algebraic equations.}

\section*{§ 30. Concept of roots. Multiple roots.}

After, in the first two sections, the algebraic magnitudes had been considered more from the formal side, where the matter was identical transformations of letter-expressions in which the letters throughout could be conceived as symbols for variable magnitudes, the numerical magnitudes now come more into the foreground.

Here, in accordance with what was fixed in the Introduction, by numbers we understand real or imaginary magnitudes of the form $a+bi$, and for illustration we represent the real magnitudes by points of a straight line and the imaginary magnitudes by points of a plane. By the absolute value of an imaginary magnitude $a+bi$ we understand $\sqrt{a^2+b^2}$ and denote it, following Weierstrass, by
\[
 |a+bi|.
\]
Let now
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=a_0x^n+a_1x^{n-1}+a_2x^{n-2}+\cdots+a_n
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
be an integral function of $x$, in which the coefficients are arbitrary real or imaginary numbers, and the first, $a_0$, is assumed different from zero. If $\alpha$ is a number which, when put for $x$, makes the function $f(x)$ equal to zero, that is, which satisfies the condition $f(\alpha)=0$, then $\alpha$ is called a root of the equation
\[
 f(x)=0.
\]
We also say briefly that $\alpha$ is a root of $f(x)$. By § 4, $f(x)$ may then be divided by $x-\alpha$ without remainder, so that one may put
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=(x-\alpha)f_1(x),
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
where $f_1(x)$ is only of the $(n-1)$st degree and has the same first coefficient $a_0$ as $f(x)$, thus
\[
 f_1(x)=a_0x^{n-1}+\cdots .
\]
The coefficients of $f_1(x)$ are given in § 4, (6).

Every root of $f_1(x)$ is therefore at the same time a root of $f(x)$; and conversely every root of $f(x)$ is either $=\alpha$ or is a root of $f_1(x)$. If one root $\alpha$ of $f(x)$ is known, then the task of finding the remaining ones is reduced to the solution of an equation of the $(n-1)$st degree.

The aim of the considerations of this section consists in proving that every function $f(x)$ of the $n$th degree has at least one root. This theorem is called the fundamental theorem of algebra. First we draw from (2) the important consequence:

\medskip
\noindent I. An equation of the $n$th degree cannot have more than $n$ roots.
\medskip

For if $f(x)$ had more than $n$ roots, then $f_1(x)$, which is only of the $(n-1)$st degree, would have more than $n-1$ roots. But an equation of the first degree has not more than one root, whence the correctness of our theorem follows by complete induction.

Sometimes one also gives it the expression:

\medskip
\noindent II. If a function $f(x)$ of the $n$th degree has more than $n$ roots, then all its coefficients must be zero.
\medskip

If $\beta$ is a root of $f_1(x)$, then one may likewise put
\[
 f_1(x)=(x-\beta)f_2(x),
\]
where $f_2(x)=a_0x^{n-2}+\cdots$ is only of the $(n-2)$nd degree. Thus, if one assumes that each of the functions obtained by this division, $f_1(x), f_2(x),\ldots$, has at least one root, one finally obtains
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=a_0(x-\alpha)(x-\beta)\cdots(x-\nu),
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
and we can therefore also express the theorem which we earlier described as the aim of our considerations as follows. It is to be proved:

\medskip
\noindent A function of the $n$th degree can be decomposed into $n$ linear factors.
\medskip

The magnitudes $\alpha,\beta,\ldots,\nu$ are then all roots of $f(x)$, and their number is therefore $n$. It is, however, not excluded that the same number occurs more than once among $\alpha,\beta,\ldots,\nu$. Then $f(x)$ would have fewer than $n$ roots, while the theorem would still remain that $f(x)$ is decomposable into $n$ linear factors. To establish agreement, it has been agreed that, if $x-\alpha$ goes into $f(x)$ several times, $\alpha$ is to be counted several times among the roots, and hence that one speaks of simple, double, triple, etc. roots.

By the concept of the derived functions [§ 13, (2)], if $\alpha$ is an arbitrary magnitude,
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=f(\alpha)+(x-\alpha)f'(\alpha)+\frac{(x-\alpha)^2}{1\cdot2}f''(\alpha)
 +\frac{(x-\alpha)^3}{1\cdot2\cdot3}f'''(\alpha)+\cdots .
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
If it is now again assumed that $f(\alpha)$ vanishes, then there results
\[
 f_1(x)=f'(\alpha)+\frac{x-\alpha}{1\cdot2}f''(\alpha)+\frac{(x-\alpha)^2}{1\cdot2\cdot3}f'''(\alpha)+\cdots,
\]
and hence
\[
 f_1(\alpha)=f'(\alpha).
\]
Thus $\alpha$ is a double root of $f(x)$ when $f'(\alpha)$ vanishes simultaneously with $f(\alpha)$. Formula (4) also shows that only under this assumption is $f(x)$ divisible by $(x-\alpha)^2$. This conclusion may be carried further and leads to the theorem:

\medskip
\noindent III. The necessary and sufficient condition that $\alpha$ be an $m$-fold root of $f(x)$ is that $f(\alpha),f'(\alpha),f''(\alpha),\ldots,f^{(m-1)}(\alpha)$ vanish, while $f^{(m)}(\alpha)$ does not vanish.

\section*{§ 31. Continuity of integral functions.}

If, as before,
\[
 f(x)=a_0x^n+a_1x^{n-1}+\cdots+a_n
\]
is an integral function of $x$, we first prove the following theorem:

\medskip
\noindent IV. $f(x)$ becomes infinitely large at the same time as $x$.
\medskip

This means that, if $C$ is an arbitrarily given positive number, the positive number $R$ can be so chosen that
\[
 |f(x)|>C
\]
as soon as the absolute value of $x$ becomes greater than $R$; or, expressed geometrically: one can describe in the $x$-plane a circle around the origin such that outside this circle the absolute value of $f(x)$ no longer falls below $C$, however large $C$ is assumed. The theorem is evident for the case of a simple power $x^n$; for if $r$ is the absolute value of $x$, then $r^n$ is the absolute value of $x^n$, and $r^n$, as soon as $r$ has become greater than $1$, is greater than $r$ and hence grows with $r$ into the infinite.

To prove the theorem in general, let us denote the absolute values of $x,a_0,a_1,a_2,\ldots,a_n$ by $r,c_0,c_1,c_2,\ldots,c_n$. Then
\begin{equation}
 |f(x)|=r^n\left|a_0+\frac{a_1}{x}+\frac{a_2}{x^2}+\cdots+\frac{a_n}{x^n}\right|,
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
and, since by the theorems proved in the Introduction the absolute value of a sum cannot be smaller than the difference and cannot be greater than the sum of the absolute values of the summands,
\[
\left|a_0+\frac{a_1}{x}+\frac{a_2}{x^2}+\cdots+\frac{a_n}{x^n}\right|
\ge c_0-\left|\frac{a_1}{x}+\frac{a_2}{x^2}+\cdots+\frac{a_n}{x^n}\right|
\ge c_0-\frac{c_1}{r}-\frac{c_2}{r^2}-\cdots-\frac{c_n}{r^n}.
\]
Since $c_0,c_1,\ldots,c_n$ are fixed constants, one can choose $R$ so large that, as soon as $r>R$,
\[
 \frac{c_1}{r}+\frac{c_2}{r^2}+\cdots+\frac{c_n}{r^n}
\]
becomes arbitrarily small, for example smaller than $\tfrac12c_0$. Then
\[
 \left|a_0+\frac{a_1}{x}+\frac{a_2}{x^2}+\cdots+\frac{a_n}{x^n}\right|>\tfrac12c_0,
\]
and hence by (2)
\[
 |f(x)|>\tfrac12c_0R^n,
\]
so that, if one assumes
\begin{equation}
 R^n>\frac{2C}{c_0},
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
one has
\[
 |f(x)|>C.
\]
To this is attached the theorem:

\medskip
\noindent V. $f(x)$ is a continuous function of $x$.
\medskip

By this the following is meant. If the absolute value of $x$ is smaller than an arbitrary finite magnitude $R$, then, however small the positive magnitude $\omega$ is assumed, one can find a positive magnitude $\varepsilon$ such that
\begin{equation}
 |f(x+h)-f(x)|<\omega
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
as long as the absolute value $\rho$ of $h$ remains smaller than $\varepsilon$, and indeed in such a way that $\varepsilon$ depends only on the choice of $\omega$, not on $x$.

By § 13, (2),
\begin{equation}
 f(x+h)-f(x)=h f'(x)+\frac{h^2}{1\cdot2}f''(x)+\cdots+h^n a_0.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
If now we assume the absolute value of $h$ smaller than $1$, then the absolute value of the magnitude in parentheses
\begin{equation}
 f'(x)+\frac{h}{1\cdot2}f''(x)+\cdots+h^{n-1}a_0
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
is smaller than a certain non-zero number $K$ which one obtains if in (6) one replaces $h$ by $1$, the coefficients $a_0,a_1,\ldots,a_{n-1}$ by their absolute values and $x$ by $R$, because thereby every individual term of the sum (6) is replaced by its absolute value or by a larger number. If one chooses $\varepsilon$ so small that
\[
 \varepsilon K<\omega,
\]
then by (5) the requirement (4) is fulfilled as long as $\rho<\varepsilon$.

We can give the theorem of the continuity of the function $f(x)$ a somewhat different expression, which is important for what follows. By the theorem that the absolute value of a sum of two terms lies, in magnitude, between the sum and the difference of the absolute values of the terms (cf. Introduction, p. 19), we obtain from (5), if we put
\[
 \varphi(h)=h f'(x)+\frac{h^2}{1\cdot2}f''(x)+\cdots+h^n a_0,
\]
\[
 |f(x)|-|\varphi(h)|<|f(x+h)|<|f(x)|+|\varphi(h)|,
\]
or
\[
 -|\varphi(h)|<|f(x+h)|-|f(x)|<|\varphi(h)|.
\]
Now, if $\rho<\varepsilon$, then $|\varphi(h)|<\omega$, and we can therefore also say:

\medskip
\noindent VI. The absolute value of the difference
\[
 |f(x+h)|-|f(x)|
\]
is smaller than an arbitrarily given magnitude $\omega$ as soon as the absolute value of $h$ is smaller than a sufficiently small $\varepsilon$.
\medskip

From this we can still conclude, if we put $x=y+iz$ and assume $h$ either real or purely imaginary, that $|f(y+zi)|$ is, for fixed $z$, a continuous function of $y$, and for fixed $y$, a continuous function of $z$.

If the coefficients $a_0,a_1,\ldots,a_n$ and the variable $x$ are real, then $x$ may be chosen, in absolute value, so large that the sign of $f(x)$ agrees with the sign of the first term $a_0x^n$; thus, for positive $a_0$ and even $n$, it becomes positive, while for odd $n$ it becomes negative for negative $x$ and positive for positive $x$.

For in
\[
 f(x)=x^n\left(a_0+\frac{a_1}{x}+\frac{a_2}{x^2}+\cdots+\frac{a_n}{x^n}\right)
\]
one can choose $x$ so large in absolute value that the absolute value of
\[
 \frac{a_1}{x}+\frac{a_2}{x^2}+\cdots+\frac{a_n}{x^n}
\]
falls below that of $a_0$, and therefore the expression in parentheses
\[
 a_0+\frac{a_1}{x}+\frac{a_2}{x^2}+\cdots+\frac{a_n}{x^n}
\]
has the sign of $a_0$, which it then retains for every absolutely larger $x$.

\section*{§ 32. Change of sign of $f(x)$. Roots of equations of odd degree and of pure equations.}

On the basis of the theorems of the preceding paragraph we can prove the following important theorem.

\medskip
\noindent VIII. If the coefficients of $f(x)$ are real and there exist two real values $a,b$ of $x$ such that $f(a)$ and $f(b)$ have opposite signs, then between $a$ and $b$ there is at least one root $\xi$ of the equation $f(x)=0$.
\medskip

We shall assume that $a<b$ and that $f(a)$ is negative, $f(b)$ positive. We divide the numbers between $a$ and $b$ into two parts $\mathfrak A$ and $\mathfrak B$ as follows: a number $\alpha$ belongs to $\mathfrak A$ if, between $\alpha$ and $a$, including the endpoints, there is no $x$ for which $f(x)$ becomes positive; and a number $\beta$ belongs to $\mathfrak B$ if between $\beta$ and $a$ there is at least one value of $x$ for which $f(x)$ becomes positive. A third possibility is plainly not possible, and every number between $a$ and $b$ is therefore placed in $\mathfrak A$ or in $\mathfrak B$. We also count the numbers smaller than $a$ as belonging to $\mathfrak A$, and those greater than $b$ as belonging to $\mathfrak B$.

If $\alpha$ belongs to $\mathfrak A$, then every $x$ smaller than $\alpha$ also belongs to $\mathfrak A$; and if $\beta$ belongs to $\mathfrak B$, every larger $x$ belongs to $\mathfrak B$. Thus every $\beta$ is greater than every $\alpha$, and the two numerical domains $\mathfrak A,\mathfrak B$ form a cut (Introduction, p. 5), generated by a number $\xi$, such that every number $\alpha$ smaller than $\xi$ belongs to $\mathfrak A$, and every number $\beta$ greater than $\xi$ belongs to $\mathfrak B$.

If now, for some number $x_0$, the value of $f(x_0)$ is negative or positive, then by V of the preceding paragraph a positive magnitude $\varepsilon$ can be so determined that for every $x$ between $x_0-\varepsilon$ and $x_0+\varepsilon$ the values of $f(x)$ are always negative or always positive. It follows from this that $f(\xi)$ can be neither negative nor positive and must therefore be zero. For if $f(\xi)$ were negative, then $f(x)$ would also be negative between $\xi$ and $\xi+\varepsilon$; these values would therefore belong to $\mathfrak A$, although they are greater than $\xi$. If $f(\xi)$ were positive, then $f(x)$ would be positive as long as $x$ lay between $\xi-\varepsilon$ and $\xi$; these values, although smaller than $\xi$, would therefore belong to $\mathfrak B$. Both alternatives are impossible by the definition of $\xi$. The same conclusion may be drawn in quite the same way when $f(a)$ is positive and $f(b)$ negative, and so our theorem is proved.

From this we draw two important consequences. If $f(\xi)$ vanishes, then an interval
\[
 \xi-\varepsilon<x<\xi+\varepsilon
\]
can be so determined that $f(x)$ does not vanish in this interval except for $x=\xi$. Then four cases are possible with respect to the signs of $f(x)$ in the two intervals $\xi-\varepsilon<x<\xi$ and $\xi<x<\xi+\varepsilon$, which are put together in the following scheme:
\[
\begin{array}{c|cc}
&\xi-\varepsilon<x<\xi&\xi<x<\xi+\varepsilon\\
1.&+&-\\
2.&-&+\\
3.&+&+\\
4.&-&-
\end{array}
\]
If $f^{(\nu)}(x)$ is the first among the derivatives of $f(x)$ which is different from zero for $x=\xi$, then the formula [§ 13, (2)]
\[
 f(\xi+h)=\frac{h^\nu}{\Pi(\nu)}f^{(\nu)}(\xi)+\cdots
\]
shows that these four cases are distinguished by the following signs:
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
1.&\nu\text{ odd},& f^{(\nu)}(\xi)<0,\\
2.&\nu\text{ odd},& f^{(\nu)}(\xi)>0,\\
3.&\nu\text{ even},& f^{(\nu)}(\xi)>0,\\
4.&\nu\text{ even},& f^{(\nu)}(\xi)<0.
\end{array}
\]
We say that in the first case $f(x)$ passes decreasing through zero, and in the second increasing through zero. In case 3, $f(\xi)=0$ is a minimum, in case 4 a maximum.

In particular, if $f'(\xi)$ is different from zero, so that $\xi$ is a simple root, then the positive or negative sign of $f'(\xi)$ is the characteristic mark for the increase or decrease of the function $f(x)$ as $x$ passes through $\xi$.

With these aids we still prove two further important theorems.

\medskip
\noindent IX. The equation
\[
 x^n-a=0
\]
has, when $a$ is a positive number, one and only one positive root.
\medskip

For if we put
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=x^n-a,
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
then by § 31, VII, $f(x)$ is positive for sufficiently large positive $x$, and negative for $x=0$; hence there is a positive value of $x$, denoted by $\sqrt[n]{a}$, for which $f(x)$ vanishes. That there can be only one such value follows from the fact that $x^n$, as long as $x$ remains positive, increases continually with $x$.

If $n$ is odd, then also for negative $a$ there exists one and only one negative root of (1), which is proved in the same way.

\medskip
\noindent X. An equation of odd degree with real coefficients always has at least one real root.
\medskip

For by theorem VII, $f(x)$, if the degree is odd, can become both positive and negative; hence, by VIII, $f(x)=0$ has a root.

Finally we prove:

\medskip
\noindent XI. A pure equation always has a root.
\medskip

By a pure equation we understand an equation of the form
\begin{equation}
 x^n-a=0,
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
where $a$ is an arbitrary complex magnitude. That this equation always has a root for real positive $a$ has already been proved above; and for $a=0$ it is plainly satisfied by $x=0$.

The general proof can be divided into two parts; it is sufficient to assume first $n=2$, and then $n$ odd. For if the magnitude $\sqrt{a}$ and $\sqrt[n]{a}$, or $\sqrt[n]{\alpha}$ for every $\alpha$, exists, then also the existence of $\sqrt[n]{a}$ for every arbitrary $n$ is secured. It would even suffice to prove the existence of $\sqrt[n]{a}$ for the case in which $n$ is a prime number. But from this no particular advantage is at first to be drawn.

We denote the complex magnitude $a$ by $b+ci$ and put, since $x$ will in general also be complex,
\[
 x=y+iz.
\]
Then we first have to show that
\begin{equation}
 (y+iz)^2=b+ic,
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
whatever real values $b,c$ may have, can be satisfied by real $y,z$. Equation (3), however, is fulfilled if
\begin{equation}
 y^2-z^2=b,
\qquad 2yz=c,
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
and hence
\[
 (y^2+z^2)^2=b^2+c^2,
\]
therefore
\begin{equation}
 y^2+z^2=\sqrt{b^2+c^2}.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
By IX there always exists a positive square root $\sqrt{b^2+c^2}$. From (4) and (5) it now follows that
\[
 y^2=\frac{b+\sqrt{b^2+c^2}}{2},\qquad
 z^2=\frac{-b+\sqrt{b^2+c^2}}{2},
\]
and the two magnitudes $\pm b+\sqrt{b^2+c^2}$ are plainly positive, since $b^2+c^2>b^2$, hence also $\sqrt{b^2+c^2}>\sqrt{b^2}$. Thus we obtain
\[
 y=\pm\sqrt{\frac{b+\sqrt{b^2+c^2}}{2}},\qquad
 z=\pm\sqrt{\frac{-b+\sqrt{b^2+c^2}}{2}},
\]
but one sign is determined by the other through the condition
\[
 2yz=c;
\]
one of the two signs remains arbitrary, so that we obtain not merely one, but two opposite solutions of (3).

Further let $n$ be odd; we take the equation to be solved in the form
\begin{equation}
 (y+iz)^n=b+ci.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
If $c=0$, so that $a$ is real, then we can put $z=0$ and by IX obtain a real value of $x$. But if we assume $c$ different from zero, then from (6) (Introduction, p. 19) there follows
\begin{equation}
 (y-iz)^n=b-ci.
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
Multiplying the two equations (6), (7) by one another gives
\[
 (y^2+z^2)^n=b^2+c^2,
\]
and hence, by IX,
\begin{equation}
 y^2+z^2=\sqrt[n]{b^2+c^2},
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
which determines the absolute value of $x$. Furthermore, from (6) and (7) one obtains
\begin{equation}
 \varphi(y,z)=(y-iz)^n(b+ic)-(y+iz)^n(b-ci)=0.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
Now $\varphi(y,z)$ is an integral homogeneous function of the $n$th degree in the two variables $y,z$, and indeed with real coefficients, since replacing $i$ by $-i$ changes nothing in $\varphi(y,z)$. If it is ordered by descending powers of $y$, the first term is $2ciy^n$, hence, by assumption, different from zero. Putting
\[
 y=\lambda z,
\]
we obtain from (9)
\begin{equation}
 \varphi(\lambda,1)=0,
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
therefore an equation of the $n$th degree for $\lambda$, which by X certainly has a root. Once $\lambda$ is determined, it follows from (8) that
\begin{equation}
 y=\lambda\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt[n]{b^2+c^2}}{1+\lambda^2}},\qquad
 z=\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt[n]{b^2+c^2}}{1+\lambda^2}}.
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
Through this the equations (8), (9) are in fact satisfied; from (9), however, follows
\[
 \frac{(y+iz)^n}{b+ic}=\frac{(y-iz)^n}{b-ci},
\]
and from (8) that the common value of these two fractions is $+1$. Therefore we have
\[
 (y+iz)^n=\pm(b+ci),\qquad (y-iz)^n=\pm(b-ci),
\]
and thus the sign of the square root in (11) can be so determined that equations (6) and (7) are fulfilled.

\section*{§ 33. Solution of pure equations by trigonometric functions.}

The solvability of a pure equation can be shown far more completely and simply if one presupposes the trigonometric functions and their simplest properties as known. These functions are, it is true, foreign to algebra proper, and for that reason it is more satisfactory to answer the questions of principle without their aid, as has been done in the preceding and will also be done later. For application and for a more convenient visualization, however, we shall not dispense with this aid.

If, when $b,c$ are real numbers, we put
\begin{equation}
 b=r\cos\varphi,
\qquad c=r\sin\varphi,
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
then, if $r$ is assumed positive, the angle $\varphi$ is thereby determined up to a multiple of $2\pi$. It will be completely determined if we fix an interval of length $2\pi$ in which it is to lie, for example
\[
 -\pi<\varphi\le \pi.
\]
In geometrical interpretation $r,\varphi$ are the polar coordinates of the point whose rectangular coordinates are $b,c$. The absolute value of the complex magnitude $a=b+ci$ is $r$, and we shall call $\varphi$ the phase of this complex magnitude.

The product of two complex magnitudes
\[
 a=r(\cos\varphi+i\sin\varphi),\qquad
 a'=r'(\cos\varphi'+i\sin\varphi')
\]
is obtained in the form
\begin{equation}
 aa'=rr'[\cos(\varphi+\varphi')+i\sin(\varphi+\varphi')],
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
and from this, if $n$ is an arbitrary positive whole number, or even a negative one, one obtains the theorem named after Moivre,
\begin{equation}
 (\cos\varphi+i\sin\varphi)^n=\cos n\varphi+i\sin n\varphi,
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
which has led to regarding the magnitude $\cos\varphi+i\sin\varphi$ as an exponential function with imaginary exponent, and hence to setting
\[
 \cos\varphi+i\sin\varphi=e^{i\varphi}.
\]
Thereby formulas (3) and (4) take the form
\begin{equation}
 e^{i\varphi}e^{i\varphi'}=e^{i(\varphi+\varphi')},\qquad (e^{i\varphi})^n=e^{in\varphi}.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
If accordingly we put
\begin{equation}
 a=r(\cos\varphi+i\sin\varphi)
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
and by $\sqrt[n]{r}$ understand the positive value existing and completely determined by § 32, IX, whose $n$th power is $r$, then
\begin{equation}
 x=\sqrt[n]{r}\left(\cos\frac{\varphi}{n}+i\sin\frac{\varphi}{n}\right)
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
is a magnitude whose $n$th power is $a$, hence a root of the equation
\begin{equation}
 x^n=a,
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
where $n$ is understood to be an arbitrary positive whole number. But every one of the values
\begin{equation}
 x_k=\sqrt[n]{r}\left(\cos\frac{\varphi+2k\pi}{n}+i\sin\frac{\varphi+2k\pi}{n}\right)
\tag{9}
\end{equation}
satisfies the same requirement, when $k$ is an arbitrary integer. In (9), however, not more than $n$ different values are contained, namely those obtained by putting
\begin{equation}
 k=0,1,2,\ldots,n-1;
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
for if $k$ is increased by a multiple of $n$, the value of (9) is not changed, while the $n$ values (10) give only different values of $x$, because they have different phases.

Equation (8) consequently has not merely one, but $n$ different roots. The geometrical images of the values $x_k$ all lie on a circle with radius $\sqrt[n]{r}$, and are separated from one another by the angle $2\pi/n$. The first of these points is obtained by dividing the given angle $\varphi$ into $n$ equal parts. The radius $\sqrt[n]{r}$ is smaller or larger than $r$ according as $r$ is smaller or larger than $1$. The adjacent figure shows these relations for $n=3$ under the assumption $r>1$.


\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.88, line cap=round, line join=round]
  \draw (0,0) circle (2.0);
  \draw (0,0) circle (1.45);
  \draw[->] (-0.2,0)--(2.55,0);
  \fill (1.45,0) circle (1.5pt) node[above] {$x_0$};
  \fill (-0.65,1.30) circle (1.5pt) node[above right] {$x_1$};
  \fill (-0.35,-1.40) circle (1.5pt) node[below right] {$x_2$};
  \node at (1.55,1.55) {$a$};
\end{tikzpicture}\\[-0.2em]
{\small Fig. 1.}
\end{center}


One can obtain the different values $x_k$ by multiplying the first of them,
\[
 x_0=\sqrt[n]{r}\left(\cos\frac{\varphi}{n}+i\sin\frac{\varphi}{n}\right),
\]
by the different values of
\begin{equation}
 \xi_k=\cos\frac{2k\pi}{n}+i\sin\frac{2k\pi}{n}.
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
The magnitudes $\xi_k$,
\begin{equation}
 \xi_k^n=1,
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
are roots of the equation $x^n=1$ and are called the $n$th roots of unity. There are $n$, and only $n$, different values of them; for odd $n$ only one is real, namely for $k=0$, and for even $n$ two are real, namely for $k=0$ and $k=n/2$. The geometrical images of the $n$th roots of unity are the vertices of a regular $n$-gon inscribed in the circle of radius $1$. Their algebraic determination is the subject of the theory of cyclotomy. If we denote by $\varepsilon$ the value of $\xi_k$ for $k=1$, thus put
\[
 \varepsilon=\cos\frac{2\pi}{n}+i\sin\frac{2\pi}{n},
\]
then, by Moivre's theorem,
\[
 \xi_k=\varepsilon^k,
\]
so that all $n$th roots of unity are represented as powers of one among them.


\section*{\S\,34. Removal of the second term of an equation.}

The certainty of the existence of the roots of pure equations puts us in a position to prove the existence of roots for equations of the second, third, and fourth degrees, or, as one also says, for quadratic, cubic, and biquadratic equations, by reducing the determination of their roots to the solution of pure equations. We shall return to this question later from various more general points of view, but here we shall briefly discuss the older methods of solution, which, even if they give little insight into the general connection of this question, are nevertheless very simple in application. They arouse the appearance as if one were dealing with a method capable of generalization to higher equations, which is not the case. First the following general remark.

Let us, for the sake of simplicity, take in the function
\begin{equation}
f(x)=x^n+a_1x^{n-1}+a_2x^{n-2}+\cdots+a_n
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
the coefficient of $x^n$ equal to $1$, which in general is obtained by division by the coefficient of $x^n$. Then by the simple substitution
\begin{equation}
x=y-\frac{a_1}{n}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
we can transform $f(x)$ into another function $\varphi(y)$ of the $n$th degree in which the second term $y^{n-1}$ does not occur; for, by the binomial theorem,
\[
 x^n=y^n-a_1y^{n-1}+\frac{n-1}{2n}a_1^2y^{n-2}+\cdots,
\]
\[
 x^{n-1}=y^{n-1}-\frac{n-1}{n}a_1y^{n-2}+\cdots,
 \qquad x^{n-2}=y^{n-2}-\cdots,
\]
and consequently
\[
 f(x)=y^n+\left(a_2-\frac{n-1}{2n}a_1^2\right)y^{n-2}+\cdots .
\]

If we actually carry out the transformation for the cases $n=2,3,4$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rcl}
n=2, & \varphi(y)&=y^2+a,\\[0.25em]
n=3, & \varphi(y)&=y^3+ay+b,\\[0.25em]
n=4, & \varphi(y)&=y^4+ay^2+by+c,
\end{array}
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
provided that we put
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rcll}
\text{for }n=2:&& a&=-\dfrac{a_1^2}{4}+a_2,\\[0.75em]
\text{for }n=3:&& a&=-\dfrac{a_1^2}{3}+a_2,\\[0.75em]
&& b&=\dfrac{2a_1^3}{27}-\dfrac{a_1a_2}{3}+a_3,\\[0.75em]
\text{for }n=4:&& a&=-\dfrac{3a_1^2}{8}+a_2,\\[0.75em]
&& b&=\dfrac{a_1^3}{8}-\dfrac{a_1a_2}{2}+a_3,\\[0.75em]
&& c&=-\dfrac{3a_1^4}{256}+\dfrac{a_1^2a_2}{16}-\dfrac{a_1a_3}{4}+a_4.
\end{array}
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
Thus the solution of the equation $f(x)=0$ is reduced by (2) to the solution of $\varphi(y)=0$. This first gives, for $n=2$,
\[
 y=\pm\sqrt{-a},
\]
and consequently
\[
 x=-\frac{a_1}{2}\pm\sqrt{\frac{a_1^2}{4}-a_2}
   =\frac{-a_1\pm\sqrt{a_1^2-4a_2}}{2}.
\]
We therefore have two roots
\begin{equation}
\alpha=\frac{-a_1+\sqrt{a_1^2-4a_2}}{2},\qquad
\beta =\frac{-a_1-\sqrt{a_1^2-4a_2}}{2}.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}

\section*{\S\,35. Cubic equations. Cardano's formula.}

We take the equation of the third degree in the reduced form
\begin{equation}
y^3+ay+b=0
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
and introduce two new unknowns $u,v$ by putting
\begin{equation}
y=u+v.
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
Substitution in (1) gives
\begin{equation}
u^3+v^3+(3uv+a)(u+v)+b=0.
\tag{3}
\end{equation}

We determine one of the two quantities $u,v$ by the other according to the equation
\begin{equation}
3uv=-a,
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
and obtain from (3)
\begin{equation}
u^3+v^3=-b.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
But from (4) and (5), $u^3$ and $v^3$ can be determined by a square root. Namely one obtains
\begin{equation}
(u^3-v^3)^2=(u^3+v^3)^2-4u^3v^3
=b^2+\frac{4a^3}{27}.
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
Put, for abbreviation,
\begin{equation}
R=\frac{b^2}{4}+\frac{a^3}{27};
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
then
\[
 u^3-v^3=2\sqrt R.
\]
We give $\sqrt R$ one of the two signs and, according to (5), obtain
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rclcrcl}
u^3&=&-\dfrac b2+\sqrt R,&& v^3&=&-\dfrac b2-\sqrt R,\\[0.75em]
u&=&\sqrt[3]{-\dfrac b2+\sqrt R},&& v&=&\sqrt[3]{-\dfrac b2-\sqrt R},
\end{array}
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{equation}
y=\sqrt[3]{-\frac b2+\sqrt R}+\sqrt[3]{-\frac b2-\sqrt R}.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}

Multiplication of the two expressions (8) gives
\[
 u^3v^3=-\frac{a^3}{27}
\]
and shows that, if for $u$ a determinate one among the three values of the cube root is taken,
\begin{equation}
v=-\frac{a}{3u}=-\frac{a}{3\sqrt[3]{-\dfrac b2+\sqrt R}}
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
is in any case one of the three values of $\sqrt[3]{-\dfrac b2-\sqrt R}$. But it follows from (4) that only when we take this value for $v$ is equation (1) satisfied by $y=u+v$. Thus, corresponding to the three values of $u$, we obtain three roots of equation (1). From the existence of one root of the cubic equation, however, the existence of three already follows also from the fact that it has already been proved that the quadratic equation has two roots.

To derive the other two roots from the one root (9), put
\[
 \alpha=u+v
\]
and divide $y^3+ay+b$ by $y-\alpha$. The quotient whose roots are the two other roots $\beta,\gamma$ of the cubic equation (1) is
\[
 y^2+\alpha y+\alpha^2+a=0,
\]
whence, by (4) of the preceding paragraph, one finds
\[
 y=\frac{-\alpha\pm\sqrt{-3\alpha^2-4a}}{2}.
\]
If here one puts $\alpha=u+v$ and, by (4), $a=-3uv$, then it follows that
\[
 y=\frac{-(u+v)\pm\sqrt{-3(u+v)^2+12uv}}{2}
  =\frac{-(u+v)\pm\sqrt{-3}(u-v)}{2}.
\]
We therefore put
\begin{equation}
\varepsilon=\frac{-1+\sqrt{-3}}{2},
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
from which
\begin{equation}
\varepsilon^3=1,\qquad \varepsilon^2+\varepsilon+1=0,
\qquad
\varepsilon^2=\frac{-1-\sqrt{-3}}{2}
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
follows. Thus one obtains the three roots of the cubic equation (1)
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rcl}
\alpha&=&u+v,\\
\beta&=&\varepsilon u+\varepsilon^2v,\\
\gamma&=&\varepsilon^2u+\varepsilon v.
\end{array}
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
Here $\varepsilon$ is a third root of unity different from $1$, expressed algebraically without the trigonometric functions. If one substitutes $\varepsilon u$ or $\varepsilon^2u$ for $u$, and hence $\varepsilon^2v$ or $\varepsilon v$ for $v$, the three quantities $\alpha,\beta,\gamma$ are interchanged with one another.

The expression (9) for the root of a cubic equation is called Cardano's formula.

\section*{\S\,36. Cayley's expression for Cardano's formula.}

Every cube root has, as we have seen, three different values, which one obtains from one of them by multiplication by $1,\varepsilon,\varepsilon^2$. Thus each of the two quantities $u,v$, as they are defined by (8), \S\,35, also has three values, and the sum $u+v$, if one takes account only of the conditions (8), has nine different values. Of these, however, only three give solutions of the cubic equation, and only by adjoining the relation (4), \S\,35,
\begin{equation}
3uv=-a
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
are the usable values singled out from among the nine. This is a defect of Cardano's formula, which accordingly by itself does not yet suffice to give the roots of the cubic equation unambiguously. Cayley remedied this defect by the following representation. Define two new quantities $\xi,\eta$ by the equations
\begin{equation}
u=\xi^2\eta,
\qquad
v=\xi\eta^2,
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
from which, by multiplication and use of (1), one obtains
\begin{equation}
\xi\eta=\sqrt[3]{-\frac a3}.
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
Consequently, if this value is inserted in (2) and the expressions \S\,35, (8) for $u,v$ are substituted, then
\begin{equation}
\xi=\sqrt[3]{\frac{3b}{2a}+\sqrt{\frac{9b^2}{4a^2}+\frac a3}},\qquad
\eta=\sqrt[3]{\frac{3b}{2a}-\sqrt{\frac{9b^2}{4a^2}+\frac a3}}.
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
Thus, if one now puts the root $y$ of the cubic equation in the form
\begin{equation}
y=\xi\eta(\xi+\eta),
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
one obtains an expression which, when $\xi$ is replaced, independently, by $\xi,\varepsilon\xi,\varepsilon^2\xi$ and $\eta$ by $\eta,\varepsilon\eta,\varepsilon^2\eta$, assumes not nine but only the three values
\[
\begin{array}{c}
\xi^2\eta+\xi\eta^2,\\
\varepsilon\xi^2\eta+\varepsilon^2\xi\eta^2,\\
\varepsilon^2\xi^2\eta+\varepsilon\xi\eta^2.
\end{array}
\]
\footnote{Cayley, Phil. Mag. vol. XXI, 1861. Collected mathematical papers, vol. V, No. 310.}



\section*{\S\,37. The biquadratic equation.}

A similar path to that used in solving the cubic equation by Cardano's formula may be taken for the solution of the equation of the fourth degree
\begin{equation}
 y^4+ay^2+by+c=0.
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
We put
\[
 2y=u+v+w
\]
and obtain, if we write, for abbreviation,
\begin{equation}
 s=u^2+v^2+w^2,\qquad
 t=v^2w^2+w^2u^2+u^2v^2,
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
then
\[
 4y^2=s+2(vw+wu+uv),
\]
\[
 16y^4=s^2+4s(vw+wu+uv)+4t+8uvw(u+v+w).
\]
Substitution in (1) gives
\[
 s^2+4t+4as+16c+8(uvw+b)(u+v+w)
  +4(s+2a)(vw+wu+uv)=0.
\]
This equation is satisfied by the assumptions
\[
 s+2a=0,\qquad uvw+b=0,\qquad s^2+4t+4as+16c=0.
\]
With the help of the first of these three equations the third becomes
\[
 t=a^2-4c,
\]
and, putting back the values (2) for $s,t$,
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rcl}
 u^2+v^2+w^2&=&-2a,\\
 v^2w^2+w^2u^2+u^2v^2&=&a^2-4c,\\
 uvw&=&-b.
\end{array}
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
By \S\,7 these equations are satisfied if and only if $u^2,v^2,w^2$ are the roots of the cubic equation
\begin{equation}
 z^3+2az^2+(a^2-4c)z-b^2=0,
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
and if the signs of $u,v,w$ are so chosen that the last of equations (3) is satisfied. This last condition still allows four different determinations of signs, so that the four roots of the biquadratic equation are obtained in the following way:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{rcl}
2\alpha&=&u+v+w,\\
2\beta&=&u-v-w,\\
2\gamma&=&-u+v-w,\\
2\delta&=&-u-v+w.
\end{array}
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
Thus the solution of the biquadratic equation has been reduced to that of the cubic equation (4).

This equation is called a cubic resolvent of the biquadratic equation.

\section*{\S\,38. Proof of the fundamental theorem.}

We now proceed to the proof of the fundamental theorem of algebra, namely that every equation $f(x)=0$ has at least one root. We first put forward some general propositions.

1. If $S$ denotes an arbitrary system of real numbers all greater than a certain positive or negative number $C$ (that is, a system which contains no infinitely negative numbers), then there exists a lower bound for the numbers $S$.

By a lower bound $g$ one is to understand a number which is not undercut by any number of the system $S$, but which is of such a nature that, if $d$ is an arbitrarily given positive quantity, then between $g$ and $g+d$ (including the endpoints) there always lies at least one number of the system $S$, however small $d$ may be.

The proof follows directly from the possibility of a cut $(\mathfrak A,\mathfrak B)$, constructed as follows: one throws a number $\alpha$ into $\mathfrak A$ if it is not undercut by any number of the system $S$, and a number $\beta$ into $\mathfrak B$ if it is undercut by at least one number in $S$. The number $g$ determined by this cut is not undercut by any number in $S$, for otherwise there would also be numbers smaller than $g$, and therefore belonging to $\mathfrak A$, which nevertheless are undercut by numbers in $S$; while, on the other hand, every number $\beta$ lying even ever so little above $g$ belongs to $\mathfrak B$ and is therefore undercut by a number in $S$. These are precisely the characteristic marks of the lower bound.

2. If $S'$ is a part of $S$, then the numbers $S'$ also have a lower bound $g'$, and this is either equal to $g$ or greater than $g$.

For it cannot be smaller than $g$, since otherwise numbers would occur in $S'$, and therefore also in $S$, which lie below $g$.

Now let $f(x)$ be a real function of $x$ which has only finite values in the interval
\begin{equation}
 a\le x\le b
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
and which is also continuous in this interval. In agreement with \S\,31, V and VI, this means that
\begin{equation}
 f(x\pm h)-f(x)
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
falls, in absolute value, below an arbitrarily prescribed number $\eta$ throughout the interval whenever the positive $h$ is smaller than a certain number $\varepsilon$. For $x=a$ we take in (2) only the upper sign, and for $x=b$ only the lower sign, so as not to pass outside the interval with $x\pm h$.

For the values of such a function in the interval there is, by 1., a lower bound $g$, and we now prove the proposition:

3. The function $f(x)$ assumes the value $g$ for some value $\xi$ of the interval, so that the lower bound becomes a minimum of the function $f(x)$ in the interval.

The proof is as follows. By 2., the lower bound of the function values in a part of the interval (1) is either equal to $g$ or greater than $g$.

If now $f(a)=g$, what is to be proved is true. If, however, $f(a)>g$, then by the continuity of $f(x)$ one can give an interval $a\le x\le a+h$ in which $f(x)>g$ remains true, and hence the lower bound of $f(x)$ is greater than $g$.

We now construct in the interval (1) a cut $(\mathfrak A,\mathfrak B)$ of the following kind: we count a value $\alpha$ of the interval (1) as belonging to $\mathfrak A$ if the lower bound of $f(x)$ in the interval $a\le x\le \alpha$ is greater than $g$, and a value $\beta$ as belonging to $\mathfrak B$ if the lower bound in the interval $a\le x\le \beta$ is equal to $g$.

It is possible that $\mathfrak B$ consists only of the single value $b$; but then $f(b)=g$ must hold, and our assertion is fulfilled for $x=b$. For if $f(b)>g$, one could choose a quantity $g'$ between $f(b)$ and $g$, and, by continuity, an interval $b-h\le x\le b$ such that in this interval all function values $f(x)$ are greater than $g'$, so that their lower bound is equal to or greater than $g'$, and hence certainly greater than $g$. But since $b-h$ belongs to $\mathfrak A$, the lower bound is also greater than $g$ in the interval $a\le x\le b-h$, and consequently in the whole interval (1), contrary to the assumption.

If therefore $f(b)>g$, the cut $(\mathfrak A,\mathfrak B)$ defines a number $\xi$ in the interior of the interval (1), and we can now show that
\begin{equation}
 f(\xi)=g
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
must hold.

If $f(\xi)>g$ and $f(\xi)>g'>g$, then, by the continuity of $f(x)$, we can determine an interval
\[
 \xi-h\le x\le \xi+h
\]
in which all function values $f(x)$ are greater than $g'$, and hence their lower bound is greater than $g$. But since $\xi-h$ belongs to $\mathfrak A$, the lower bound of $f(x)$ in the whole interval $a\le x\le \xi+h$ is also greater than $g$, while $\xi+h$ belongs to $\mathfrak B$, and this is the contradiction.

Let $f(x)$ now be an integral rational function with arbitrary complex or real coefficients, and let the variable $x$ also be allowed to take complex values.

By \S\,31, IV, if $C$ is an arbitrary positive value, the positive quantity $R$ can be so chosen that
\begin{equation}
 |f(x)|>C
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
whenever
\begin{equation}
 |x|\ge R.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
For visualization we represent the region $(R)$ bounded by the inequality
\[
 |x|<R
\]
for the variable $x=y+iz$ by a circular disc of radius $R$ in the plane of the variable $x=y+iz$.

If we take $C$ larger than any value of $|f(x)|$ in the interior of the region $(R)$, for example larger than $|f(0)|$, then $|f(x)|$ will certainly become smaller in the interior of $(R)$ than on its boundary. Since now in the whole interior of $(R)$ the function $|f(x)|$, which for abbreviation we shall now denote by $X$, is not negative, there is a lower bound $g$ for the values of $X$, and we have to prove the proposition:

4. There exists a value $\xi$ of $x$ in the interior of the region $(R)$ such that
\begin{equation}
 |f(\xi)|=g,
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
so that here also the lower bound is a minimum.

The lower bound of $X$ in any part of the region is either equal to $g$ or greater than $g$.

A domain of magnitudes determined by the inequalities
\begin{equation}
 |x|\le R,\qquad -R\le y\le \alpha
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
is represented in our Figure 2 by the segment $(PQ\alpha Q')$, which we shall call the segment $(P,\alpha)$.

\begin{center}{\small Fig. 2.}\end{center}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.0]
\draw (0,0) circle (2.0);
\draw[->] (-2.35,0)--(2.35,0) node[right] {$y$};
\draw[->] (0,-2.25)--(0,2.25) node[above] {$z$};
\node[below left] at (0,0) {$O$};
\node[left] at (-2,0) {$P$};
\draw[dashed] (-1.18,-1.62)--(-1.18,1.62);
\draw[dashed] (-0.92,-1.78)--(-0.92,1.78);
\draw[dashed] (-0.62,-1.90)--(-0.62,1.90);
\node[above left] at (-1.18,1.62) {$Q$};
\node[below left] at (-1.18,-1.62) {$Q'$};
\node[above left] at (-0.92,1.78) {$M$};
\node[below left] at (-0.92,-1.78) {$M'$};
\node[above left] at (-0.62,1.90) {$N$};
\node[below left] at (-0.62,-1.90) {$N'$};
\node[below] at (-1.18,0) {$\alpha$};
\node[below] at (-0.92,0) {$\eta$};
\node[below] at (-0.62,0) {$\beta$};
\draw (-0.92,0.08)--(-0.92,-0.08);
\node[right] at (-0.80,0.85) {$\xi$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

We now first determine a value $\eta$ of $y$ by a cut $(\mathfrak A,\mathfrak B)$ as follows: a number $\alpha$ between $-R$ and $+R$ is admitted into $\mathfrak A$ if the lower bound of $X$ in the segment $(P,\alpha)$ is greater than $g$, and a value $\beta$ is admitted into $\mathfrak B$ if the lower bound of $X$ in the segment $(P,\beta)$ is equal to $g$. This cut $(\mathfrak A,\mathfrak B)$ defines a number $\eta$ with the property that the lower bound of $X$ in the region $(P,\gamma)$ is greater than $g$ if $\gamma<\eta$, and equal to $g$ if $\gamma>\eta$.

Now, by \S\,31, VI,
\[
 |f(\eta+iz)|
\]
is a continuous function of $z$ in the interval
\begin{equation}
 -\sqrt{R^2-\eta^2}\le z\le \sqrt{R^2-\eta^2},
\tag{8}
\end{equation}
which is denoted in Figure 2 by $M,M'$.

Thus by 3. this function receives, for some value $\zeta$ of $z$ in this interval, a minimum value $\gamma$, so that, if
\[
 \xi=\eta+i\zeta
\]
is put,
\begin{equation}
 |f(\xi)|=\gamma.
\tag{9}
\end{equation}

It is now further easy to see that $\gamma=g$ must be the case; for since $g$ is the lower bound of all values of $X$ within $(R)$, $\gamma$ cannot first of all be smaller than $g$.

But $\gamma$ also cannot be greater than $g$; for all values which $X$ assumes on the segment $MM'$ are $\ge\gamma$. If, however, $\gamma>g$, then one can choose a quantity $g'$ such that $\gamma>g'>g$, and, by the continuity of $X$ proved in \S\,31, VI, numbers $\alpha,\beta$ can be so determined that
\[
 \alpha<\eta<\beta
\]
and such that in the whole region $(P,\beta)-(P,\alpha)=(\alpha,\beta)$ ($QNN'Q'$ in Figure 2) $X$ remains greater than $g'$. Consequently the lower bound of $X$ in $(\alpha,\beta)$ is greater than $g$. Since now the lower bound of $X$ in $(P,\alpha)$ is also greater than $g$, it is also greater than $g$ in $(P,\beta)$, which is composed of $(P,\alpha)$ and $(\alpha,\beta)$; but $\beta$ belongs to $\mathfrak B$, and this would give a contradiction with the definition of $\mathfrak B$.

Thus there remains only the possibility that $\gamma=g$, and proposition 4 is thereby proved; namely, there is a value $\xi$ in the interior of $(R)$ for which
\[
 |f(\xi)|=g
\]
holds, so that $|f(x)|$ attains a minimum value. We now prove:

5. If $\alpha$ is any value of $x$ for which $f(\alpha)$ is different from zero, then $h$ can be chosen so that
\begin{equation}
 |f(\alpha+h)|<|f(\alpha)|
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
results.

It then follows that, if $f(\xi)$ were different from zero, $g$ could not be the minimum of the function $|f(x)|$; but since this has been proved, one must have
\begin{equation}
 f(\xi)=0,
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
and $\xi$ is a root of the equation $f(x)=0$. The fundamental theorem will therefore be proved.

In the proof of 5. we make use of the theorem already proved (\S\,32), that a pure equation always has a root.

Some of the derived functions $f'(\alpha),f''(\alpha),\ldots$ may vanish; the last, $f^{(n)}(\alpha)$, which is equal to $\Pi(n)a_0$, is however different from zero. Thus suppose that $f'(\alpha),f''(\alpha),\ldots,f^{(m-1)}(\alpha)$ vanish, but $f^{(m)}(\alpha)$ does not vanish. We then have, by \S\,13,
\begin{equation}
 f(\alpha+h)=f(\alpha)+\frac{h^m}{\Pi(m)}f^{(m)}(\alpha)
 +\frac{h^{m+1}}{\Pi(m+1)}f^{(m+1)}(\alpha)+\cdots .
\tag{12}
\end{equation}

We choose, as is always possible by \S\,31, V, a positive number $\varepsilon$ so that
\begin{equation}
\left|\frac{h}{m+1}\frac{f^{(m+1)}(\alpha)}{f^{(m)}(\alpha)}
+\frac{h^2}{(m+1)(m+2)}\frac{f^{(m+2)}(\alpha)}{f^{(m)}(\alpha)}+\cdots\right|<1
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
whenever
\begin{equation}
 |h|<\varepsilon,
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
and a positive proper fraction $\delta$ such that
\begin{equation}
 \delta |f(\alpha)|<\left|\frac{\varepsilon^m f^{(m)}(\alpha)}{\Pi(m)}\right|,
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
which, if $f(\alpha)$ does not vanish, is likewise possible. We then determine $h$ (on the basis of \S\,32) from the equation
\begin{equation}
 \frac{h^m f^{(m)}(\alpha)}{\Pi(m)}=-\delta f(\alpha),
\tag{16}
\end{equation}
whence, by (15),
\[
 |h|<\varepsilon
\]
follows. From (12), when the value from (16) is substituted for $h^m$, we now get
\[
 f(\alpha+h)=(1-\delta)f(\alpha)
 -\delta f(\alpha)\left(\frac{h}{m+1}\frac{f^{(m+1)}(\alpha)}{f^{(m)}(\alpha)}
 +\frac{h^2}{(m+1)(m+2)}\frac{f^{(m+2)}(\alpha)}{f^{(m)}(\alpha)}+\cdots\right),
\]
and hence
\begin{align}
 |f(\alpha+h)|\le{}& |f(\alpha)|(1-\delta)\notag\\
 &+\delta |f(\alpha)|\left|\frac{h}{m+1}\frac{f^{(m+1)}(\alpha)}{f^{(m)}(\alpha)}
 +\frac{h^2}{(m+1)(m+2)}\frac{f^{(m+2)}(\alpha)}{f^{(m)}(\alpha)}+\cdots\right|.
\tag{17}
\end{align}
By (13), therefore,
\begin{equation}
 |f(\alpha+h)|<|f(\alpha)|.\tag{18}
\end{equation}
Thus the proof of the fundamental theorem is completed.


\section*{\S\,39. Algorithm for the calculation of the roots.}

The proof given in the preceding paragraph for the existence of a root of an algebraic equation, although it leaves nothing to be desired in conclusiveness, nevertheless still has the defect that it does not make clear the steps by which a root can be calculated by a convergent computational procedure. We therefore add the following considerations, intended to remedy this defect, even if only theoretically. They also give a second proof of the fundamental theorem, due in its essentials to Lipschitz (\emph{Lehrbuch der Analysis}, vol. I, \S\,61 ff.; I owe a simplification to a communication of Dedekind).

If the two integral rational functions $f(x)$ and $f'(x)$ have a common divisor, then this can be found and removed by rational operations according to \S\,6. We may therefore assume that $f(x)$ and $f'(x)$ have no common divisor, that is, that they do not vanish simultaneously for any value of $x$.

We now assume that the calculation of the roots has already succeeded for a function of degree $n-1$, and accordingly suppose $f'(x)$ resolved into linear factors. Thus, if
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=x^n+a_1x^{n-1}+a_2x^{n-2}+\cdots,
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
 f'(x)=nx^{n-1}+(n-1)a_1x^{n-2}+\cdots
      =n(x-\beta_1)(x-\beta_2)\cdots(x-\beta_{n-1}),
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
where $\beta_1,\beta_2,\ldots,\beta_{n-1}$ are to be regarded as known numbers, some of which may also be identical. By our assumption, the absolute values
\begin{equation}
 |f(\beta_1)|,\\ |f(\beta_2)|,\ldots, |f(\beta_{n-1})|,
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
which we denote by
\begin{equation}
 b_1,b_2,\ldots,b_{n-1},
\tag{4}
\end{equation}
are all different from zero; let the notation be so chosen that $b_1$ is the smallest among them, or at least exceeds none of the others. By theorem 5 of \S\,38 a value $\alpha$ of $x$ can then be determined so that the absolute value $a$ of $f(\alpha)$ is smaller than $b_1$, hence
\begin{equation}
 a<b_1,b_2,\ldots,b_{n-1}.
\tag{5}
\end{equation}

We now bound a domain $G$ for the variable $x$ in such a way that outside this domain the absolute value of $f(x)$ is always greater than $a$, so that $G$ contains all points $x$ for which $|f(x)|<a$ (but also other points). By \S\,31, IV this is possible in the following way. About the origin as centre draw a circle $(R)$ of sufficiently large radius $R$ containing the points $\alpha,\beta_1,\beta_2,
\ldots,\beta_{n-1}$, and cut out of this circle the points $\beta_1,\beta_2,
\ldots,\beta_{n-1}$, at which $|f(x)|$ is certainly greater than $a$, by circular envelopes of sufficiently small but non-zero radii $\rho_1,\rho_2,
\ldots,\rho_{n-1}$, so that within all these circles $(\rho_1),(\rho_2),\ldots,(\rho_{n-1})$ the absolute value $|f(x)|$ remains greater than $a$ (\S\,31, V). None of these circles then encloses the point $\alpha$, for at that point $|f(x)|=a$. The connected piece of surface bounded by the circles $(R),(\rho_1),\ldots,(\rho_{n-1})$ is the domain $G$.

We now determine a sequence of numbers
\[
 \alpha,\alpha',\alpha'',\alpha''',\ldots
\]
in such a way that the absolute values of $f(\alpha),f(\alpha'),f(\alpha''),\ldots$, which we denote by
\[
 a,a',a'',a''',\ldots,
\]
always decrease. The points so determined all remain in the interior of the domain $G$, since for them $|f(x)|<a$.

For this we use a procedure quite similar to that used for the proof of theorem 5 in \S\,38, but simplified by the fact that $f'(\alpha)$ is already different from zero. If $\delta$ denotes a positive proper fraction, still to be determined more precisely, we put in the expansion
\begin{equation}
 f(\alpha+h)=f(\alpha)+h f'(\alpha)+\frac{h^2}{1\cdot2}f''(\alpha)+\cdots
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 h=-\delta\frac{f(\alpha)}{f'(\alpha)},
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
and obtain
\begin{align}
 f(\alpha+h)={}&(1-\delta)f(\alpha)
 +\frac{\delta^2 f(\alpha)^2 f''(\alpha)}{1\cdot2\,f'(\alpha)^2}
 -\frac{\delta^3 f(\alpha)^3 f'''(\alpha)}{1\cdot2\cdot3\,f'(\alpha)^3}+\cdots .
\tag{8}
\end{align}

Now by (2)
\[
 f'(\alpha)=n(\alpha-\beta_1)(\alpha-\beta_2)\cdots(\alpha-\beta_{n-1});
\]
therefore, if the absolute values of $\alpha-\beta_1,\alpha-\beta_2,
\ldots,\alpha-\beta_{n-1}$ are denoted by $r_1,r_2,
\ldots,r_{n-1}$,
\[
 |f'(\alpha)|=nr_1r_2\cdots r_{n-1}.
\]
Since $\alpha$ lies outside the circle of radius $\rho_i$ drawn about $\beta_i$, we have $r_1>\rho_1,r_2>\rho_2,\ldots$, and consequently
\[
 |f'(\alpha)|>n\rho_1\rho_2\cdots\rho_{n-1}.
\]
Thus $|f'(\alpha)|$ is greater than a positive number $k$ independent of the position of $\alpha$ in $G$. It follows that one can choose a sufficiently large positive number $Q$, likewise independent of the position of $\alpha$ and of the proper fraction $\delta$, so that
\begin{equation}
 \left|\frac{f(\alpha)^2f''(\alpha)}{1\cdot2\,f'(\alpha)^2}
 -\delta\frac{f(\alpha)^3f'''(\alpha)}{1\cdot2\cdot3\,f'(\alpha)^3}+\cdots\right|<Q .
\tag{9}
\end{equation}

One obtains $Q$, for example, by replacing in the denominators on the left of (9) $f'(\alpha)$ by $k$, replacing $\delta$ by $1$, replacing all the other factors by their absolute values, replacing finally the absolute value of $\alpha$ by the greater value $R$, and then making the number so obtained arbitrarily larger. From (8) one obtains
\begin{equation}
 |f(\alpha+h)|<(1-\delta)|f(\alpha)|+\delta^2Q.
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
Assume now $Q$ so chosen that, for every $\alpha$ in $G$,
\[
 2Q>|f(\alpha)|=a,
\]
which is plainly compatible with the above determination. We may then put
\begin{equation}
 \delta=\frac{a}{2Q},\qquad
 h=-\frac{a f(\alpha)}{2Q f'(\alpha)},
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
and, denoting $\alpha+h$ by $\alpha'$, and denoting $|f(\alpha')|$ by $a'$, obtain from (10)
\begin{equation}
 a'<a\left(1-\frac{a}{4Q}\right).
\tag{12}
\end{equation}

By the same procedure we derive from $\alpha',a'$ a second system $\alpha'',a''$, then from $\alpha'',a''$ a third system $\alpha''',a'''$, and so on; for the sequence of decreasing positive numbers $a,a',a'',\ldots$ we obtain the inequalities
\begin{equation}
 a'<a\left(1-\frac{a}{4Q}\right),\quad
 a''<a'\left(1-\frac{a'}{4Q}\right),\quad
 a'''<a''\left(1-\frac{a''}{4Q}\right),\ldots .
\tag{13}
\end{equation}
We prove that this sequence approaches the limit zero. If this were not the case, a positive number $\omega$ could be given such that all $a^{(\nu)}$ remain above $\omega$; the differences
\[
 \frac{a^{(\nu)}}{4Q}
\]
would then all be greater than the positive proper fraction $\omega/(4Q)$, and from (13) it would follow that
\[
 a'<a\theta,
 \quad a''<a'\theta,
 \quad a'''<a''\theta,
 \quad\ldots,
\]
where $0<\theta<1$. By multiplication one gets
\begin{equation}
 a^{(\nu)}<a\theta^\nu.
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
This is a contradiction, since $\theta^\nu$ becomes infinitely small as $\nu$ increases without bound. The result so obtained is expressed by
\begin{equation}
 \lim a^{(\nu)}=0.
\tag{15}
\end{equation}

If the inequalities (13) are written in the form
\[
 a-a'>\frac{a^2}{4Q},\qquad
 a'-a''>\frac{a'^2}{4Q},\qquad\ldots,
\]
and added from the $(\mu+1)$-st to the $(\nu+1)$-st, one obtains
\[
 (a^{(\mu)})^2+(a^{(\mu+1)})^2+\cdots+(a^{(\nu)})^2
 <4Q\bigl(a^{(\mu)}-a^{(\nu+1)}\bigr),
\]
and therefore, when $\mu$ and $\nu$ both tend to infinity,
\begin{equation}
 \lim\bigl((a^{(\mu)})^2+(a^{(\mu+1)})^2+\cdots+(a^{(\nu)})^2\bigr)=0.
\tag{16}
\end{equation}

For the sequence of numbers $\alpha,\alpha',\alpha'',\ldots$ the law of formation resulting from (11) is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \alpha'&=\alpha-\frac{a f(\alpha)}{2Q f'(\alpha)},\\
 \alpha''&=\alpha'-\frac{a' f(\alpha')}{2Q f'(\alpha')},\\
 &\hspace{1.5em}\vdots\\
 \alpha^{(\nu)}&=\alpha^{(\nu-1)}-
 \frac{a^{(\nu-1)} f(\alpha^{(\nu-1)})}{2Q f'(\alpha^{(\nu-1)})}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{17}
\end{equation}
If an arbitrary number of these equations are added, from the $(\mu+1)$-st to the $(\nu+1)$-st, then
\begin{equation}
 \alpha^{(\mu)}-\alpha^{(\nu+1)}=
 \sum_{r=\mu}^{\nu}\frac{a^{(r)}f(\alpha^{(r)})}{2Q f'(\alpha^{(r)})}.
\tag{18}
\end{equation}
Since all the $|f'(\alpha^{(r)})|$ are at least equal to $k$, it follows for the absolute value of this difference that
\begin{equation}
 |\alpha^{(\mu)}-\alpha^{(\nu+1)}|<
 \frac{(a^{(\mu)})^2+(a^{(\mu+1)})^2+\cdots+(a^{(\nu)})^2}{2Qk},
\tag{19}
\end{equation}
and hence, by (16),
\begin{equation}
 \lim |\alpha^{(\mu)}-\alpha^{(\nu+1)}|=0.
\tag{20}
\end{equation}

This says (according to the concept of a sequence of numbers explained in the Introduction, p. 15) that the numbers $\alpha,\alpha',\alpha'',\ldots$ approach a definite limit, which we shall denote by $\xi$. From the continuity of the function $f(x)$ it then follows easily that $f(\xi)=0$; for if $|f(\xi)|>0$, then, since the $\alpha^{(\nu)}$ come arbitrarily close to $\xi$, the absolute values $a^{(\nu)}$ of $f(\alpha^{(\nu)})$ could not sink below every positive value, which is precisely what we have proved for them.


\section*{\S\,40. Continuity of the roots.}

We close this section with the proof of the theorem:

\begin{center}
\emph{The roots of an algebraic equation are continuous functions of the coefficients.}
\end{center}

We have first to explain the meaning of this theorem.

Let
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=x^n+a_1x^{n-1}+a_2x^{n-2}+\cdots
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
be an integral rational function of $x$ of degree $n$. By what has been proved in the preceding paragraphs, $f(x)$ can be decomposed into $n$ linear factors, some of which may be equal to one another. Combining equal factors and denoting the roots by $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots$, we put
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=(x-\alpha)^a(x-\beta)^b(x-\gamma)^c\cdots,
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
where $a,b,c,\ldots$ are positive whole numbers whose sum is equal to $n$.

The roots $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots$ will change with the coefficients $a_1,a_2,a_3,\ldots$; the degree of their multiplicity can also become another one.

We denote the changes of $a_1,a_2,
\ldots$ by $\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2,\ldots$ and put
\begin{align}
 \varphi(x)&=\varepsilon_1x^{n-1}+\varepsilon_2x^{n-2}+\cdots,
\tag{3}\\
 f(x)+\varphi(x)&=f_1(x).
\tag{4}
\end{align}
We surround the points $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,
\ldots$ by regions of arbitrary smallness, but in such a way that these regions exclude one another; for instance, we enclose the points $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,
\ldots$ by circular peripheries with radii $\rho,\rho',\rho'',\ldots$, and denote these regions by $(\rho),(\rho'),(\rho''),\ldots$.

If the absolute values of $\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2,
\ldots$ lie below sufficiently small values, then we can prove first of the function $f_1(x)$
\begin{center}
that it has no roots outside the regions $(\rho),(\rho'),(\rho''),\ldots$,
\end{center}
and secondly,
\begin{center}
that the number of roots of $f_1(x)$ within $(\rho)$ is exactly $a$, within $(\rho')$ exactly $b$, within $(\rho'')$ exactly $c$, and so on.
\end{center}
In the last part of the theorem, however, it must be observed that, if $f_1(x)$ has multiple roots, these must be counted according to their multiplicity.

We construct, according to \S 31, IV, in the $x$-plane a circle with radius $R$ and with the origin as centre, which encloses the regions $(\rho),(\rho'),(\rho''),\ldots$, so that outside this circle no more roots of $f_1(x)$ lie, and we denote by $G$ the region lying within this circle, but outside $(\rho),(\rho'),(\rho''),\ldots$. It must also be remarked that $R$ can be assumed independently of $\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2,\ldots$, so long as a definite upper bound is fixed for the absolute values of these quantities.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.95]
\draw (0,0) circle (2.25);
\draw (-0.9,0.55) circle (0.32);
\draw (1.25,0.25) circle (0.32);
\draw (-1.05,-1.05) circle (0.32);
\node at (-0.9,0.55) {$\alpha$};
\node at (1.25,0.25) {$\beta$};
\node at (-1.05,-1.05) {$\gamma$};
\node at (-1.38,0.50) {$\rho$};
\node at (1.72,0.35) {$\rho'$};
\node at (-0.58,-1.00) {$\rho''$};
\node at (0,-0.45) {$G$};
\node at (1.95,1.05) {$R$};
\node at (0,2.55) {Fig. 4.};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

If now $x$ is a point of the region $G$, then the absolute value of $x-\alpha$ is greater than $\rho$, and consequently, by (2),
\begin{equation}
 |f(x)|>\rho^a\rho'^b\rho''^c\cdots .
\tag{5}
\end{equation}
If now $\alpha_1$ is a root of $f_1(x)$, then from (4) it follows that
\begin{equation}
 f(\alpha_1)=-\varphi(\alpha_1),
\tag{6}
\end{equation}
and $\alpha_1$ therefore cannot lie in the region $G$ if one takes care that everywhere within $G$
\begin{equation}
 |\varphi(x)|<\rho^a\rho'^b\rho''^c\cdots,
\tag{7}
\end{equation}
which is always possible by sufficient diminution of the upper bound of $\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2,
\ldots$. Thus the first part of our assertion is proved, namely that, when the inequality (7) is satisfied, no roots of $f_1(x)$ lie outside the regions $(\rho),(\rho'),(\rho''),\ldots$.

To prove the second part, we put
\begin{align}
 f(x)&=\psi(x)(x-\alpha)^a,
\tag{8}\\
 \psi(x)&=(x-\beta)^b(x-\gamma)^c\cdots .
\tag{9}
\end{align}
We now choose a positive number $A$, which shall however be independent of $\rho$, in such a way that, as long as $x$ lies in the interior or on the periphery of the region $(\rho)$,
\begin{equation}
 |\psi(x)|>A \qquad (\text{for } |x-\alpha|\le \rho)
\tag{10}
\end{equation}
remains true. Such a number is obtained, for example, if $l$ is taken equal to half the least of the distances $(\alpha,\beta),(\alpha,\gamma),\ldots$ and one puts
\[
 A=l^{b+c+\cdots};
\]
then the requirement expressed in (10) is fulfilled at least as long as $\rho$ is smaller than $l$. If only one point $\alpha$ is present, then $\psi(x)=1$ is to be put, and for $A$ any proper fraction can be chosen.

Let now $\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots$ be the roots of $f_1(x)$ within $(\rho)$, and let $a_1,a_2,
\ldots$ be the degrees of their multiplicity; and let $\beta_1,\beta_2,
\ldots$, $b_1,b_2,
\ldots$, $\gamma_1,\gamma_2,
\ldots$, $c_1,c_2,
\ldots$ have the same meaning for the regions $(\rho'),(\rho''),\ldots$. Then
\begin{equation}
 n=a_1+a_2+\cdots+b_1+b_2+\cdots+c_1+c_2+\cdots,
\tag{11}
\end{equation}
since the total number of all roots must be equal to $n$. The function $f_1(x)$ can then be represented as
\begin{equation}
 f_1(x)=\psi_1(x)(x-\alpha_1)^{a_1}(x-\alpha_2)^{a_2}\cdots,
\tag{12}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \psi_1(x)=(x-\beta_1)^{b_1}(x-\beta_2)^{b_2}\cdots
          (x-\gamma_1)^{c_1}(x-\gamma_2)^{c_2}\cdots .
\tag{13}
\end{equation}

Now we can determine a positive number $B$ independent of $\rho,\rho',\rho'',\ldots$ such that, as long as $x$ remains within or on the boundary of $(\rho)$,
\begin{equation}
 |\psi_1(x)|<B \qquad (\text{for } |x-\alpha|\le \rho).
\tag{14}
\end{equation}
For example, one can choose a magnitude $L$ which is greater than twice the distance of the point $\alpha$ from one of the points $\beta,\gamma,\ldots$ and in every case greater than $1$, and then assume
\[
 B>L^n .
\]
Now from (4) it follows that
\begin{equation}
 |f(x)|<|f_1(x)|+|\varphi(x)|,
\tag{15}
\end{equation}
hence by (8) and (12)
\begin{equation}
 |\psi(x)|\,|x-\alpha|^a<|\psi_1(x)|\,|x-\alpha_1|^{a_1}\,|x-\alpha_2|^{a_2}\cdots+|\varphi(x)|.
\tag{16}
\end{equation}
If we now assume $x$ on the periphery of $\rho$, and therefore put
\[
 |x-\alpha|=\rho,
\]
then
\[
 |x-\alpha_1|<2\rho,\\qquad |x-\alpha_2|<2\rho,\ldots,
\]
and consequently, by (10) and (16),
\begin{equation}
 \rho^a A < (2\rho)^{a_1+a_2+\cdots}B+|\varphi(x)|.
\tag{17}
\end{equation}
We now choose the upper bounds for the coefficients $\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2,
\ldots$ of $\varphi$ so small that
\[
 |\varphi(x)|<\rho^aA'
\]
where $A'$ denotes a number which is smaller than $A$; then from (17) it follows that
\begin{equation}
 A-A'<2^{a}\rho^{-a+a_1+a_2+\cdots}B.
\tag{18}
\end{equation}
But, for sufficiently small $\rho$, this would no longer be possible if $a$ were greater than the sum $a_1+a_2+\cdots$. Hence
\begin{equation}
 a\le a_1+a_2+\cdots .
\tag{19}
\end{equation}
In the same way one proves that
\begin{equation}
 b\le b_1+b_2+\cdots,
 \qquad
 c\le c_1+c_2+\cdots,
 \qquad \ldots .
\tag{20}
\end{equation}
But since the sums of the left sides as well as of the right sides in the inequalities (19), (20) must be equal to $n$, only equality signs can hold, and therefore
\[
 a=a_1+a_2+\cdots,
 \quad b=b_1+b_2+\cdots,
 \quad c=c_1+c_2+\cdots,
 \quad \ldots,
\]
whereby the second part of our theorem is also proved.

We shall add to the theorem just proved the following remark concerning the case of multiple roots.

The necessary and sufficient condition that $f(x)=0$ should have multiple roots at all is that $f(x)$ and $f'(x)$ should have a common factor. Therefore, if the algorithm of the greatest common divisor is applied to these two functions according to the principles developed in the first section, the condition for common factors is obtained by setting the last constant remainder equal to zero, in the form of an equation between the coefficients,
\[
 F(a_1,a_2,\ldots,a_n)=0,
\]
where $F$ is an integral rational function of the arguments $a_1,a_2,
\ldots,a_n$, which is called the discriminant of $f$, and whose properties and mode of formation will be treated more fully in the next section. In any case, since equations of degree $n$ without multiple roots do exist at all, $F$ cannot vanish identically.

Consider now
\[
 F(a_1+\varepsilon_1,a_2+\varepsilon_2,
\ldots,a_n+\varepsilon_n).
\]
This function too, if $a_1,a_2,
\ldots,a_n$ are definite and $\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2,
\ldots,\varepsilon_n$ are indeterminate numbers, will not vanish identically; and one can dispose of the $\varepsilon$'s so that its value comes out different from zero, even when any arbitrary upper bound has been fixed for the absolute values of the $\varepsilon$'s.\footnote{This will only later be rigorously justified. For the time being we presuppose it in these preliminary remarks, on which no further conclusions will be founded.}

One therefore sees from these considerations how an $a$-fold root $\alpha$ of $f(x)$, under continuous variation of the coefficients, radiates apart into $a$ simple roots, so that from this point of view too one is justified in considering the $a$-fold root as having arisen from the coincidence of $a$ simple roots.

If we do not assume the coefficient of the highest power of $x$ equal to $1$, and therefore examine the equation in the form
\begin{equation}
 a_0x^n+a_1x^{n-1}+a_2x^{n-2}+\cdots+a_{n-1}x+a_n=0,
\tag{21}
\end{equation}
then, if $a_n=0$, one or more of the roots are equal to zero, and equation (21) can be divided by $x$ or by a higher power of $x$, whereby an equation of correspondingly lower degree arises in which the last term is different from zero. We shall therefore assume from the outset that $a_n$ is different from zero, and now put in (21)
\begin{equation}
 x=\frac1y.
\tag{22}
\end{equation}
By multiplication by $y^n$ and division by $a_n$, (21) then passes into
\begin{equation}
 y^n+\frac{a_{n-1}}{a_n}y^{n-1}+\cdots+\frac{a_1}{a_n}y+\frac{a_0}{a_n}=0,
\tag{23}
\end{equation}
and we can apply to the roots of this equation the theorem just stated, by assuming $a_0=0$, so that one of the roots of (23) vanishes. We thus obtain the theorem:

One can take $a_0$ in the equation (21) so small that one of its roots becomes large beyond all bounds, while the others differ arbitrarily little from the roots of the equation of degree $n-1$
\[
 a_1x^{n-1}+a_2x^{n-2}+\cdots+a_{n-1}x+a_n=0.
\]
This is also expressed by saying that, as $a_0$ vanishes, one of the roots of (21) becomes infinite.

If $a_1$ also vanishes, or $a_1$ and $a_2$, and so on, two or three roots become infinite.

This justifies the expression sometimes used, that an equation of degree $n$ passes, by the becoming-infinite of one root, into an equation of degree $n-1$.

\section*{Fourth Section. Symmetric functions.}

\section*{\S\,41. Concept of symmetric functions. Symmetric fundamental functions.}

In this section we consider whole functions, of arbitrary degree, in an arbitrary number $n$ of variables
\[
 \alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n.
\]

Such a function is called symmetric if it remains unchanged when the variables $\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n$ are subjected to an arbitrary permutation; and it is such symmetric functions whose properties and laws of formation we now have to learn more precisely.

In order that a function $\Phi(\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n)$ be symmetric, it is sufficient that it not be changed when any two of the arguments $\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n$ are interchanged, since by \S\,18 all permutations can be formed by a succession of transpositions.

The function $\Phi$ will in general not be homogeneous, but will contain terms of different dimensions. If, however, one collects all terms of the same dimension, every $\Phi$ can be represented as a sum of homogeneous functions of different degrees; and, if $\Phi$ is to be symmetric, then each homogeneous constituent of a definite degree must by itself be symmetric, since the dimensions of the terms are not changed by the permutations of the variables.

Accordingly we may confine ourselves to the consideration of homogeneous symmetric functions, from which all the others can be put together.

After this it is easy to give the general form of a symmetric function. We obtain it if, in one term of such a function,
\[
 \alpha_1^{\nu_1}\alpha_2^{\nu_2}\cdots\alpha_n^{\nu_n},
\]
as in the formation of determinants, we permute the lower indices in all possible ways and take the sum of all terms so formed. Such a function may be called an elementary constituent of a symmetric function. If we take several such elementary constituents, multiply them by arbitrary factors independent of the $\alpha$'s, and add them, then we obtain the most general symmetric function. The number of terms of one of these elementary constituents is $n!$ when the exponents $\nu_1,\nu_2,\ldots,\nu_n$ are all different; but if one and the same exponent $\nu$ occurs several times, the permutations which give no different terms are to be omitted. For example, for three variables, if $\nu_1,\nu_2,\nu_3$ are different, an elementary constituent is
\[
 \alpha_1^{\nu_1}\alpha_2^{\nu_2}\alpha_3^{\nu_3}
+\alpha_1^{\nu_1}\alpha_2^{\nu_3}\alpha_3^{\nu_2}
+\alpha_1^{\nu_2}\alpha_2^{\nu_1}\alpha_3^{\nu_3}
+\alpha_1^{\nu_2}\alpha_2^{\nu_3}\alpha_3^{\nu_1}
+\alpha_1^{\nu_3}\alpha_2^{\nu_1}\alpha_3^{\nu_2}
+\alpha_1^{\nu_3}\alpha_2^{\nu_2}\alpha_3^{\nu_1},
\]
whereas, if $\nu_2=\nu_3$,
\[
 \alpha_1^{\nu_1}\alpha_2^{\nu_2}\alpha_3^{\nu_2}
+\alpha_1^{\nu_2}\alpha_2^{\nu_1}\alpha_3^{\nu_2}
+\alpha_1^{\nu_2}\alpha_2^{\nu_2}\alpha_3^{\nu_1}.
\]

The simplest example of a symmetric function is the sum of the variables
\[
 \alpha_1+\alpha_2+\alpha_3+\cdots+\alpha_n.
\]
Likewise the product
\[
 \alpha_1\alpha_2\cdots\alpha_n
\]
belongs here.

These two are the extreme cases of a series of symmetric functions which we call the symmetric fundamental functions and obtain as follows.

The product
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=(x-\alpha_1)(x-\alpha_2)\cdots(x-\alpha_n)
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
is, whatever $x$ may be, provided only that $x$ is independent of the $\alpha$'s, a symmetric function of $\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n$. If therefore we carry out the multiplication of the several factors and arrange according to powers of $x$, then the coefficients of the separate powers of $x$ are likewise symmetric functions. For they change no more under interchange of the $\alpha$'s than does the function $f(x)$ (\S\,1). We put
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=x^n+a_1x^{n-1}+a_2x^{n-2}+\cdots+a_{n-1}x+a_n.
\tag{2}
\end{equation}

The functions $a_1,a_2,\ldots,a_n$ are the coefficients of the algebraic equation whose roots are $\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_n$. They are called the symmetric fundamental functions.
