\tag{17}       2^{n-1}\prod_{v=0}^{n-1}\cos\left(\varphi+\frac{2v\pi}{n}\right)
        =\cos n\varphi.
\]
Since both sides are continuous functions of $\varphi$, these formulas also remain valid in the initially excluded case where $\varphi$ is a multiple of $\pi$.

Under the product sign we may replace $v$ by $vm$ if $m$ is any integer relatively prime to $n$, since then $vm$ runs through a complete residue system modulo $n$ along with $v$. Separating off the value $v=0$, and then letting $v$ run through both the positive and negative integers of absolute value less than $n:2$, we get from (17), for every odd $n$,
\[
\tag{18}       2^{n-1}\prod_{v=1}^{(n-1)/2}
\cos\left(\varphi+\frac{2vm\pi}{n}\right)
\cos\left(\varphi-\frac{2vm\pi}{n}\right)
=\frac{\cos n\varphi}{\cos\varphi}.
\]
Putting $\varphi=0$ and taking the square root, this gives
\[
\tag{19}       2^{(n-1)/2}\prod_{v=1}^{(n-1)/2}\cos\frac{2vm\pi}{n}=\pm1,
\]
where the sign remains undetermined for the moment. If, however, in (18) we let $\varphi$ pass to $\pi:2$, the right side, which assumes the indeterminate form $0:0$, obtains the limiting value $(-1)^{(n-1)/2}n$. Thus we again obtain the formula derived in the preceding paragraph for prime $n$, now generally for every odd $n$:
\[
\tag{20}       2^{(n-1)/2}\prod_{v=1}^{(n-1)/2}\sin\frac{2vm\pi}{n}=\pm\sqrt n,
\]
and for $m=1$ the positive sign is to be taken.

The function $B_n$, which has degree $n-1$ in $x$, vanishes when $n\varphi$, but not $\varphi$, is a multiple of $\pi$; that is, for
\[
\tag{21}       x=2\cos\frac{h\pi}{n},
\]
where $h$ is an integer not divisible by $n$. First suppose that $n$ is even. Then we obtain all the distinct values contained in (21) if we put $m$ for any number relatively prime to $n$ and take
\[
\tag{22}       h=m,2m,3m,\ldots,(n-1)m.
\]
For
\[
        \cos\frac{h\pi}{n}=\cos\frac{h'\pi}{n}
\]
can occur only if either $h+h'$ or $h-h'$ is a multiple of $2n$, which cannot happen when $h$ and $h'$ are both taken from the sequence (22).

Among the values (21), one, namely the one corresponding to $h=mn:2$, is zero, while the remaining values are pairwise opposite. Hence for even $n$
\[
\tag{23}       B_n(x)=x\prod_{v=1}^{(n-2)/2}\left(x^2-4\cos^2\frac{v m\pi}{n}\right).
\]
If $n$ is odd, then $B_n(x)$ depends only on $x^2$; hence two values in (21) are opposite to each other for $h$ and $h+n$. Taking again $m$ relatively prime to $n$, and putting
\[
        h=2m,4m,6m,\ldots,(n-1)m,
\]
one obtains in (21) neither two equal nor two opposite values, and thus all distinct values of $x^2$. Therefore, for odd $n$,
\[
\tag{24}       B_n(x)=\prod_{v=1}^{(n-1)/2}
\left(x^2-4\cos^2\frac{2vm\pi}{n}\right).
\]
Now put in this formula
\[
        x=2\cos\varphi,
        \qquad x^2=4(1-\sin^2\varphi),
        \qquad \cos^2\frac{2vm\pi}{n}=1-\sin^2\frac{2vm\pi}{n}.
\]
It can then be written
\[
\tag{25}       \frac{\sin n\varphi}{\sin\varphi}
=(-1)^{(n-1)/2}2^{n-1}\prod_{v=1}^{(n-1)/2}
\left(\sin^2\varphi-\sin^2\frac{2vm\pi}{n}\right),
\]
from which formula (20) again follows for $\varphi=0$.

Since $\cos n\varphi$ vanishes for odd $n$ when
\[
        \cos\varphi=\pm\sin\frac{2vm\pi}{n},
\]
we have thereby also found the factorization of the function $A_n(x)$ in this case. For odd $n$,
\[
\tag{26}       A_n(x)=x\prod_{v=1}^{(n-1)/2}
\left(x^2-4\sin^2\frac{2vm\pi}{n}\right).
\]
The quantities
\[
\tag{27}       \omega=\sin^2\frac{2vm\pi}{n}
\]
are therefore the roots of an algebraic equation of degree $(n-1):2$,
\[
        \Phi_n(x)=0.
\]
We obtain the function $\Phi_n(x)$ by replacing the variable $x^2$ in the function $A_n(x):x$ by a new variable $4x$ and dividing by $2^{n-1}$. Hence [formula (11)]
\[
\tag{28}\begin{aligned}
\Phi_n(x)&=\prod(x-\omega)\\
&=x^{(n-1)/2}-\frac n4 x^{(n-3)/2}
+\frac{n(n-3)}{1\cdot2\cdot16}x^{(n-5)/2}-\cdots,
\end{aligned}
\]
and (25) can be written
\[
\tag{29}       \frac{\sin n\varphi}{\sin\varphi}
=(-1)^{(n-1)/2}2^{n-1}\prod(\sin^2\varphi-\omega).
\]


\sect{138}{Determination of Signs. Quadratic Residues}

In the preceding paragraph we derived two formulas in which signs still remained to be determined, namely
\[
\tag{1}
2^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\prod_{\nu=1}^{(n-1)/2}\cos\frac{2\nu m\pi}{n}=\pm1,
\]
\[
\tag{2}
2^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\prod_{\nu=1}^{(n-1)/2}\sin\frac{2\nu m\pi}{n}=\pm\sqrt n,
\]
where $m$ is relatively prime to the odd number $n$, and $\nu$ runs through the sequence
\[
(\nu)\qquad 1,2,\ldots,\frac{n-1}{2}.
\]
We shall retain the sign $\nu$ for the numbers of this sequence.

Every integer gives, on division by $n$, as remainder one of the numbers $\nu$, or $0$, or $n-\nu$. Instead of $n-\nu$ we may, if negative remainders are allowed, choose $-\nu$. Thus, if the numbers divisible by $n$ are disregarded, one of the numbers $\pm\nu$ remains as remainder. We call this the absolutely least remainder, in distinction from the least remainder in the ordinary sense, which is taken from the numbers $1,2,\ldots,n-1$.

Let $m$ be relatively prime to $n$. Consider the sequence
\[
(m\nu)\qquad m,\;2m,\;3m,\ldots,\frac{n-1}{2}m
\]
and form for each term the absolutely least remainder $\rho$:
\[
(\rho)\qquad \pm\nu_1,\;\pm\nu_2,\;\pm\nu_3,\ldots,
       \pm\nu_{\frac12(n-1)}.
\]
Among these numbers $\rho$ no two equal numbers and no two opposite numbers occur. For if the sum or difference of two such numbers were zero, then for two different numbers $\nu,\nu'$ from $(\nu)$ the number
\[
        m(\nu\pm\nu')
\]
would be divisible by $n$, hence $\nu\pm\nu'$ itself would be divisible by $n$, which is impossible since both $\nu$ and $\nu'$ are less than $n:2$. Thus, apart from sign and order, the totality of the numbers $(\rho)$ agrees with the totality of the numbers $(\nu)$.

In formulas (1) and (2), because of the periodicity of sine and cosine, we may replace $\nu m$ by $\rho$; and since $\cos(-\rho)=\cos\rho$, we may in formula (1) also replace $m\nu$ by $\nu$. Hence the sign in (1) is independent of $m$. To determine it we use the fact that the cosine of an angle in the first quadrant is positive and in the second quadrant negative. Thus the sign in (1) is positive or negative according as an even or odd number of the angles
\[
        \frac{2\nu\pi}{n}
\]
lies in the second quadrant, or equivalently according as an even or odd number of the numbers $\nu$ lies between $\frac n4$ and $\frac n2$.

If $x$ is not an integer, write $E(x)$ for the greatest integer smaller than $x$, so that $x$ lies between $E(x)$ and $E(x)+1$. The number of integers lying between $x$ and $y$ is then $E(y)-E(x)$. We must therefore decide whether
\[
        E\left(\frac n2\right)-E\left(\frac n4\right)
\]
is even or odd. The four cases are
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
 n=8k+1, & E(n/2)=4k,   & E(n/4)=2k,\\
 n=8k+3, & E(n/2)=4k+1, & E(n/4)=2k,\\
 n=8k+5, & E(n/2)=4k+2, & E(n/4)=2k+1,\\
 n=8k+7, & E(n/2)=4k+3, & E(n/4)=2k+1.
\end{array}
\]
Therefore the positive sign is to be taken in (1) when $n$ is of the form $8k+1$ or $8k+7$, and the negative sign when $n$ is of the form $8k+3$ or $8k+5$. In the first cases
\[
        \frac{n^2-1}{8}
\]
is even, in the last two it is odd. Hence
\[
\tag{3}
2^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\prod_{\nu=1}^{(n-1)/2}
       \cos\frac{2\nu m\pi}{n}
   =(-1)^{\frac{n^2-1}{8}}.
\]

In formula (2) the sign depends on $m$: it is positive or negative according as an even or odd number of negative signs occurs among the numbers $(\rho)$. To express the dependence of this sign on $m$ and $n$ we write (2) in the form
\[
\tag{4}
2^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\prod_{\nu=1}^{(n-1)/2}
       \sin\frac{2\nu m\pi}{n}
   =\left(\frac mn\right)\sqrt n,
\]
where
\[
        \left(\frac mn\right)=\pm1.
\]
The symbol $\left(\frac mn\right)$ was introduced into number theory from another direction and in a more special form by Legendre, and was generalized by Jacobi. We shall call it the Legendre symbol. Its meaning for number theory will appear presently; first we have the following laws.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item The value of $\left(\frac mn\right)$ is $+1$ or $-1$ according as the number of negative signs among the absolutely least remainders of $m\nu$ is even or odd.

For $m=1$ the positive sign holds in (2), and hence
\[
        \left(\frac 1n\right)=+1.
\]

\item If $m$ is replaced by $-m$, the sign of every factor on the left of (4) changes. Since the number of factors is $\frac{n-1}{2}$, we get
\[
\tag{3'}
        \left(\frac{-m}{n}\right)=(-1)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\left(\frac mn\right),
\]
and for $m=1$
\[
\tag{4'}
        \left(\frac{-1}{n}\right)=(-1)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}.
\]

\item If $2m$ is put for $m$, and the formula
\[
        \sin\frac{4\nu m\pi}{n}
        =2\sin\frac{2\nu m\pi}{n}\cos\frac{2\nu m\pi}{n}
\]
is used, then (3) gives
\[
\tag{5}
        \left(\frac{2m}{n}\right)=(-1)^{\frac{n^2-1}{8}}\left(\frac mn\right),
\]
and for $m=1$
\[
\tag{6}
        \left(\frac 2n\right)=(-1)^{\frac{n^2-1}{8}}.
\]

\item If $m$ is changed by a multiple of $n$, all factors of the product (4) remain unchanged; consequently
\[
\tag{7}
        \left(\frac mn\right)=\left(\frac {m'}n\right),
        \qquad\text{if }m\equiv m'\pmod n.
\]
\end{enumerate}

Now let not only $n$ but also $m$ be odd and positive. We apply formula (29) of §\,137, with $n$ there replaced by $m$:
\[
\tag{5}
\frac{\sin m\varphi}{\sin\varphi}
=(-1)^{\frac{m-1}{2}}2^{m-1}\prod_\beta(\sin^2\varphi-\beta),
\]
where $\beta$ runs through all roots of $\Phi_m(x)=0$ of the form
\[
\tag{6}
        \beta=\sin^2\frac{2\mu\pi}{m}.
\]
If $m'$ is relatively prime to $n$, then
\[
\tag{7}
        \alpha=\sin^2\frac{2\nu m'\pi}{n}
\]
runs through all roots of $\Phi_n(x)=0$. Putting in (5)
\[
\tag{8}
        \varphi=\frac{2\nu m'\pi}{n}
\]
and taking the product over all $\nu$, we obtain
\[
\tag{9}
\frac{\prod_\nu \sin\frac{2\nu m m'\pi}{n}}
     {\prod_\nu \sin\frac{2\nu m'\pi}{n}}
=(-1)^{\frac{(m-1)(n-1)}4}
2^{\frac{(m-1)(n-1)}2}\prod_\alpha\prod_\beta(\alpha-\beta).
\]
The right hand side is wholly independent of $m'$. Thus the value of the left hand side is the same as for $m'=1$. Substituting the values of the products from (4) gives
\[
        \left(\frac{mm'}n\right):\left(\frac{m'}n\right)
        =
        \left(\frac mn\right):\left(\frac 1n\right),
\]
or
\[
        \left(\frac{mm'}n\right)=
        \left(\frac mn\right)\left(\frac{m'}n\right).
\]
At first $m$ and $m'$ were assumed positive and odd; but by the preceding laws the same result remains valid when $m$ or $m'$ is negative or even, provided only that both are relatively prime to $n$.

Assuming again that $m$ is odd and positive, (4) and (9) give
\[
\tag{10}
\left(\frac mn\right)
=(-1)^{\frac{(m-1)(n-1)}4}
2^{\frac{(m-1)(n-1)}2}\prod_\alpha\prod_\beta(\alpha-\beta).
\]
If $m$ and $n$ are interchanged here, each factor in the double product on the right changes sign; everything else remains the same. The number of these factors is
\[
        \frac{n-1}{2}\cdot\frac{m-1}{2},
\]
and therefore
\[
\tag{9'}
        \left(\frac mn\right)
        =(-1)^{\frac{(m-1)(n-1)}4}\left(\frac nm\right).
\]
Equivalently, $\left(\frac mn\right)$ is equal to $\left(\frac nm\right)$ if at least one of the odd numbers $m,n$ is of the form $4k+1$, and is the negative of $\left(\frac nm\right)$ if both are of the form $4k+3$.

This famous theorem is known as the law of reciprocity. The proof given here is due to Eisenstein. With its help, and using 5 and 6, one can determine the value of $\left(\frac mn\right)$ very quickly, proceeding as if one were computing the greatest common divisor of $m$ and $n$.

From 8 and 9 follows one last theorem, valid when $n$ and $n'$ are positive odd numbers relatively prime to $m$:
\[
\tag{10'}
        \left(\frac mn\right)\left(\frac m{n'}\right)
        =
        \left(\frac m{nn'}\right).
\]
Its correctness follows, for positive odd $m$, from the congruence
\[
\tag{7'}
 (nn'-1)-(n-1)-(n'-1)
 = (n-1)(n'-1)\equiv0\pmod 4,
\]
after applying formula 9 and then 8 to both sides of 10; when $m$ is even or negative it follows from 4 and 6.

These theorems reduce $\left(\frac mn\right)$ to a product of values $\left(\frac qp\right)$ with $p,q$ prime. In practice, however, $\left(\frac mn\right)$ is more easily computed directly by 9, without this decomposition.

We shall now learn another meaning of $\left(\frac mp\right)$ when $p$ is an odd prime. This is the meaning through which Legendre originally introduced the symbol. If all integers $x$ not divisible by $p$ are squared and their residues modulo $p$ are examined, only $\frac{p-1}{2}$ distinct residues are obtained: congruent numbers give the same square residues, and opposite numbers also give the same square residues. All these residues are certainly obtained by squaring
\[
(\nu)\qquad 1,2,3,\ldots,\frac{p-1}{2}.
\]
These squares are all distinct modulo $p$, since for distinct $\nu,\nu'$ in this range
\[
        \nu^2-\nu'^2=(\nu-\nu')(\nu+\nu')
\]
cannot be divisible by $p$. Thus among
\[
(s)\qquad 1,2,3,\ldots,p-1
\]
one half occur as residues of $x^2$, and the other half do not. The former numbers, together with all numbers congruent to them modulo $p$, are called the quadratic residues of $p$, the latter the quadratic non-residues of $p$.

If $m$ is a quadratic residue, then the congruence
\[
        x^2\equiv m\pmod p
\]
is soluble, and by 7 and 8
\[
        \left(\frac mp\right)
        =\left(\frac{x^2}p\right)
        =\left(\frac xp\right)^2=+1.
\]
Conversely, the result from §\,135 says that as $m$ runs through $1,2,\ldots,p-1$, the symbol $\left(\frac mp\right)$ has the positive and negative signs equally often. Hence the negative sign occurs precisely for the quadratic non-residues. Thus:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=11]
\item If $p$ is an odd prime and $m$ is not divisible by $p$, then
\[
        \left(\frac mp\right)=+1\quad\hbox{or}\quad -1
\]
according as $m$ is a quadratic residue or a quadratic non-residue of $p$.
\end{enumerate}

This contains all the principal theorems on quadratic residues; for example: the product of two quadratic residues, or of two non-residues, is a residue, while the product of a residue and a non-residue is a non-residue.

\section*{Third Book.\\Algebraic Quantities}

\section*{Thirteenth Section.\\Galois Theory}

\sect{139}{The Concept of a Field}

A system of numbers is called a number field if it is so complete and closed in itself that the four fundamental arithmetical operations, addition, subtraction, multiplication and division, performed on any numbers of the system, except division by zero, always lead to numbers belonging to the same system. This concept, which divides kinds of numbers from a natural point of view, was introduced by Dedekind. It is of the highest importance for algebra, and it is useful to have a significant and expressive name for it.

The concept of a number field can be extended to all quantities with which one can calculate according to the rules of the four species; in particular to rational functions of any variables.

We call a function field a system of functions of one or more variables with the property that the four arithmetical operations can be carried out in the system without restriction and always lead to a definite function of the same system, with the single exception of division by zero. The variables may be wholly independent of one another; but it is also possible that certain relations between them have been fixed and must be observed in calculation.

A function of a function field counts as zero only when it vanishes identically, that is, for all values of the variables under consideration.

Since for the moment we do not wish to restrict the discussion, we shall simply speak of fields, and call the objects with which the operations are to be carried out, whether numbers or functions, quantities or elements of the field.

A field is therefore a system of quantities complete enough that its quantities can be added, subtracted, multiplied and divided. If all quantities of one field are contained in a second field, the first is called a divisor of the second. If $\Omega$ is a divisor of $\Omega'$, we shall also say that $\Omega'$ is a field over $\Omega$.

Strictly speaking, the number zero alone forms a field by itself; for brevity we shall exclude this case once and for all. The nearest example is then the totality of rational numbers. This field is a divisor of every other field: every field contains a non-zero quantity $\omega$, hence also $\omega:\omega=1$, and therefore all integers and, by division, all fractions.

Other examples of number fields are the complex numbers $x+yi$, with $i=\sqrt{-1}$ and rational $x,y$; the totality of real numbers; and the totality of all complex numbers $x+yi$. As an example of a function field one may take all rational functions of one variable, including the constants, where the constants are restricted to a given number field or are left unrestricted.

\sect{140}{Adjunction}

Let a field of quantities be denoted by $\Omega$. If a quantity $\alpha$ not contained in it is added, a new system
\[
        \Omega,\alpha
\]
arises, but not yet a field. To obtain a field, one must add all quantities that can be derived by combining $\alpha$ with the numbers of $\Omega$ by the fundamental operations. Thus one obtains an enlarged field $\Omega'$ containing both $\alpha$ and $\Omega$, and completely determined by them. We shall call it the field $\Omega,\alpha$.

This addition of a new quantity $\alpha$ to a field $\Omega$ is called adjoining, and one says that $\Omega'$ arises from $\Omega$ by adjunction of $\alpha$.

To $\Omega'$ one may again adjoin a quantity $\alpha'$ and obtain a third field $\Omega''$ containing $\Omega,\alpha,\alpha'$. But one may also adjoin two or more numbers to $\Omega$ simultaneously and obtain the same field. One may also adjoin one field to another field and obtain a field containing the quantities of both. Adjoining to a field one of its divisors produces no new field.

For example, adjoining $i=\sqrt{-1}$ to the field $\mathfrak R$ of rational numbers gives the field $\mathfrak C$ of complex numbers $x+yi$ with rational $x,y$. Adjoining a variable $u$ to $\mathfrak R$ gives the field of rational functions in $u$ with rational coefficients; adjoining $u$ and $i$ to $\mathfrak R$ gives rational functions in $u$ with coefficients in $\mathfrak C$.

A field of quantities is also often called, following Kronecker, a domain of rationality. This expression is useful when one wants to indicate that, in a given problem, the quantities of that field are to be considered known or rational.

\sect{141}{Functions in a Field}

We shall often consider integral rational functions of one variable $x$,
\[
\tag{1}
        f(x)=a_0x^m+a_1x^{m-1}+\cdots+a_{m-1}x+a_m,
\]
whose coefficients belong to a given field $\Omega$. We shall call these functions contained in $\Omega$, or simply functions in $\Omega$. They are not to be confused with functions that belong to the field $\Omega$ when $\Omega$ itself is a function field.

Earlier we saw that such a function has $m$ roots $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_m$ when the coefficients have fixed numerical values. If the coefficients are independent variables, the roots vary continuously with them as long as the coefficients remain in a sufficiently small region where the discriminant does not vanish.

Let $\Omega$ be any field and $f(x)$ a function in $\Omega$. Then either $f(x)$ can be decomposed into factors of lower degree with coefficients in $\Omega$, or it cannot. In the first case it is reducible, in the second irreducible, in $\Omega$. If the field is clear, one often simply says reducible or irreducible.

A linear function is irreducible in every field. A product of several functions of the form (1) is reducible. A function irreducible in $\Omega$ may become reducible in an enlarged field; for instance, adjoining a root $\alpha$ of $f(x)=0$ makes $f(x)$ have the linear factor $x-\alpha$.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\Roman*.,leftmargin=2.8em]
\item An irreducible function $f(x)$ can have no common divisor with another function $F(x)$ whose coefficients belong to the same field, unless $F(x)$ is divisible by $f(x)$.

Indeed, the greatest common divisor of $F(x)$ and $f(x)$ can be found by rational operations and therefore belongs to $\Omega$. Since $f(x)$ has no divisor in $\Omega$ except itself and constants, this greatest common divisor is either constant or $f(x)$.

It follows that an irreducible function cannot have repeated factors, for then it would have a common divisor with its derivative $f'(x)$.

\item If $f(x)$ is irreducible and $F(x)$ vanishes for one root of $f(x)=0$, then it vanishes for all roots of $f(x)=0$.

\item If $F(x)$ is of lower degree than the irreducible function $f(x)$ and vanishes for one root of $f(x)$, then $F(x)$ must vanish identically.
\end{enumerate}

Integral rational functions of several variables $f(x,y,z,\ldots)$ with coefficients in $\Omega$ are also called functions in $\Omega$. They are reducible or irreducible according as they can or cannot be decomposed into factors that are themselves functions in $\Omega$ and actually contain some of the variables. For several variables one must distinguish between functions that are not decomposable at all, functions decomposable only with coefficients outside $\Omega$, and functions decomposable into functions in $\Omega$; only the last kind will be called reducible in $\Omega$.

The fundamental theorem on irreducible functions established earlier may now be stated in this setting:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\Roman*.,leftmargin=2.8em,start=4]
\item A reducible function in $\Omega$ can be decomposed into irreducible factors in only one way, if functions differing only by a constant factor are not regarded as different.
\end{enumerate}

\sect{142}{Algebraic Fields}

Let $\Omega$ be a field and $F(x)$ a function in $\Omega$. The equation
\[
\tag{1}
        F(x)=0
\]
is called an equation in $\Omega$. It is reducible or irreducible according as $F(x)$ is reducible or irreducible. By adjoining to $\Omega$ a root $\alpha$ of such an equation, if $\alpha$ is not already in $\Omega$, one obtains a new field $\Omega(\alpha)$, called an algebraic field over $\Omega$.

If $\beta,\gamma,\ldots$ are roots of other equations in $\Omega$, or other roots of the same equation, simultaneous adjunction gives fields
\[
        \Omega(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots).
\]
This apparent enlargement of the concept will soon be reduced to adjunction of a single element.

The equation (1) with root $\alpha$ may be reducible. Among the irreducible factors of $F(x)$ there is one, denoted $f(x)$, that vanishes at $x=\alpha$. Taking its leading coefficient to be $1$, it is uniquely determined. Thus
\[
\tag{2}
        x^n+a_1x^{n-1}+a_2x^{n-2}+\cdots+a_{n-1}x+a_n=0,
\]
with $a_i\in\Omega$. The degree $n$ of this equation is also called the degree of the field $\Omega(\alpha)$.

Every quantity $\Theta$ of $\Omega(\alpha)$ is obtained from $\alpha$ and quantities in $\Omega$ by the four operations; hence it can be written as a rational function of $\alpha$,
\[
\tag{3}
        \Theta=\frac{\chi(\alpha)}{\psi(\alpha)},
\]
where $\chi(x)$ and $\psi(x)$ are functions in $\Omega$. Since $\psi(\alpha)\ne0$, $\psi(x)$ is not divisible by $f(x)$; and since $f(x)$ is irreducible, $\psi(x)$ is relatively prime to $f(x)$. Therefore functions $Q(x)$ and $\varphi(x)$ in $\Omega$, with $\varphi$ of degree at most $n-1$, may be chosen so that
\[
\tag{4}
        Q(x)f(x)+\varphi(x)\psi(x)=\chi(x).
\]
Putting $x=\alpha$ gives
\[
\tag{5}
        \frac{\chi(\alpha)}{\psi(\alpha)}=\varphi(\alpha).
\]

Thus every element of $\Omega(\alpha)$ has one and only one representation
\[
        \Theta=c_0+c_1\alpha+c_2\alpha^2+\cdots+c_{n-1}\alpha^{n-1}
\]
with $c_i\in\Omega$. Uniqueness follows because the difference of two such expressions is a function of degree lower than $n$ vanishing at $\alpha$, and therefore is identically zero.

When the quantities of $\Omega$ are the given rational quantities and $\Omega(\alpha)$ is derived from them, we may say simply that $\Omega(\alpha)$ consists of all rational functions of $\alpha$.

\sect{143}{Multiple Adjunction}

Before investigating $\Omega(\alpha)$ more closely, we use a simple but important lemma.

If $\Phi_1(x,y,z,\ldots),\Phi_2(x,y,z,\ldots),\ldots$ are integral rational functions whose coefficients are arbitrary and which do not all vanish identically, then rational numerical values can be assigned to the variables in infinitely many ways so that none of the functions $\Phi_i$ vanishes.

For one variable this is immediate. The general case follows by induction: write the functions as polynomials in one variable, choose the remaining variables so that not all coefficients vanish, and then choose the last variable to avoid the finitely many zeros.

Now let $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots$ be algebraic quantities satisfying equations
\[
\tag{1}
        A(x)=0,\qquad B(x)=0,\qquad C(x)=0,\ldots
\]
in $\Omega$, with no repeated roots. Every element of $\Omega(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots)$ is an integral rational function in $\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots$ with coefficients in $\Omega$.

Form a linear function
\[
        \xi=x\alpha+y\beta+z\gamma+\cdots.
\]
If $\alpha',\beta',\gamma',\ldots$ is any other choice of one root from each equation, put
\[
        \xi'=x\alpha'+y\beta'+z\gamma'+\cdots,
\]
and similarly form $\xi'',\xi''',\ldots$. If the degrees of $A,B,C,\ldots$ are $a,b,c,\ldots$, the number of these quantities is
\[
        m=abc\cdots.
\]
The differences $\xi-\xi'$, $\xi-\xi''$, and so on are non-identically-zero linear functions of $x,y,z,\ldots$. By the lemma we may choose rational values of these variables so that the $m$ quantities $\xi,\xi',\xi'',\ldots$ are all distinct.

The elementary symmetric functions of these $\xi$'s are symmetric in the roots of each of the equations (1), and so are rationally expressible in their coefficients, hence belong to $\Omega$. Thus
\[
\tag{2}
        F(t)=(t-\xi)(t-\xi')(t-\xi'')\cdots
\]
is an equation in $\Omega$ without repeated roots and with one root $\xi$.

If $\Theta\in\Omega(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots)$, let $\Theta',\Theta'',\ldots$ be the corresponding values obtained by replacing the roots by $\alpha',\beta',\gamma',\ldots$ and so on. Then
\[
        F(t)\left(
        \frac{\Theta}{t-\xi}+
        \frac{\Theta'}{t-\xi'}+
        \frac{\Theta''}{t-\xi''}+\cdots
        \right)=\psi(t)
\]
is an integral rational function of degree $m-1$ in $t$ with coefficients in $\Omega$. Putting $t=\xi$ gives
\[
\tag{3}
        \Theta=\frac{\psi(\xi)}{F'(\xi)}.
\]
Therefore every $\Theta$ belongs to $\Omega(\xi)$, while every element of $\Omega(\xi)$ plainly belongs to $\Omega(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots)$. Hence
\[
        \Omega(\xi)=\Omega(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots).
\]

\sect{144}{Primitive and Imprimitive Fields}

We may now restrict attention to fields $\Omega(\alpha)$ generated by one root. Let
\[
\tag{1}
        F(x)=0
\]
be the relevant equation without repeated factors, of degree $m$, with roots
\[
\tag{2}
        \alpha,\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_{m-1}.
\]
If $F(x)$ is reducible in $\Omega$, there is exactly one irreducible factor $f(x)$ of $F(x)$ for which $\alpha$ is a root:
\[
\tag{3}
        f(x)=0.
\]
Let its degree be $n$, and let its roots be
\[
\tag{4}
        \alpha,\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots,\alpha_{n-1}.
\]
Then the degree of $\Omega(\alpha)$ is $n$. The fields
\[
\tag{5}
        \Omega(\alpha),\Omega(\alpha_1),\ldots,\Omega(\alpha_{n-1})
\]
are called the fields conjugate to $\Omega(\alpha)$.

Every $\Theta\in\Omega(\alpha)$ can be written as $\Theta=\chi(\alpha)$, with $\chi$ a polynomial in $\Omega$ of degree at most $n-1$. Substituting the other roots gives
\[
\tag{6}
        \Theta=\chi(\alpha),\quad
        \Theta_1=\chi(\alpha_1),\quad\ldots,\quad
        \Theta_{n-1}=\chi(\alpha_{n-1}),
\]
the conjugates of $\Theta$. Every symmetric function of these conjugates belongs to $\Omega$. In particular, their sum
\[
\tag{7}
        S(\Theta)=\Theta+\Theta_1+\Theta_2+\cdots+\Theta_{n-1}
\]
is called the trace of $\Theta$, and their product
\[
\tag{8}
        N(\Theta)=\Theta\Theta_1\Theta_2\cdots\Theta_{n-1}
\]
is called the norm of $\Theta$. Conjugate quantities have the same traces and norms.

The product
\[
\tag{9}
        (t-\Theta)(t-\Theta_1)\cdots(t-\Theta_{n-1})=\Phi(t)
\]
is a polynomial of degree $n$ in $\Omega$, with roots $\Theta$ and its conjugates. Therefore every element of $\Omega(\alpha)$ is a root of an equation of degree $n$ in $\Omega$, whose other roots are its conjugates.

If $\Phi(t)$ is reducible, let $\varphi(t)$ be one irreducible factor. If $\varphi(\Theta)=0$, then $\varphi[\chi(x)]$ and $f(x)$ have a common root. Since $f$ is irreducible, $\varphi[\chi(x)]$ vanishes for every root $\alpha_i$. Thus either all conjugates of $\Theta$ are distinct, in which case $\Phi$ itself is irreducible, or the distinct conjugates split into $n_1$ values each repeated $n_2$ times:
\[
\tag{10}
        \varphi(t)=(t-\Theta)(t-\Theta_1)\cdots(t-\Theta_{n_1-1}),
\]
\[
\tag{11}
        \Phi(t)=\varphi(t)^{n_2},
\]
\[
\tag{12}
        n=n_1n_2.
\]
Thus $\Phi(t)$ is either irreducible or a power of an irreducible polynomial.

An element $\Theta$ of $\Omega(\alpha)$ whose conjugates are all distinct, and which therefore satisfies an irreducible equation of degree $n$, is called a primitive element of the field. Infinitely many such primitive elements can be found.

Every element $\omega$ of $\Omega(\alpha)$ can be rationally expressed in terms of any primitive element $\Theta$. Indeed, if $\omega,\omega_1,\ldots,\omega_{n-1}$ and $\Theta,\Theta_1,\ldots,\Theta_{n-1}$ are the corresponding conjugates, then
\[
        \Phi(t)=(t-\Theta)(t-\Theta_1)\cdots(t-\Theta_{n-1})
\]
and
\[
        \Phi(t)\left(
        \frac{\omega}{t-\Theta}+
        \frac{\omega_1}{t-\Theta_1}+\cdots+
        \frac{\omega_{n-1}}{t-\Theta_{n-1}}
        \right)=\Psi(t)
\]
with $\Psi(t)\in\Omega[t]$. Setting $t=\Theta$ gives
\[
        \omega=\frac{\Psi(\Theta)}{\Phi'(\Theta)}.
\]
Hence $\Omega(\Theta)=\Omega(\alpha)$.

If $\Theta$ is not primitive, not every element of $\Omega(\alpha)$ can be expressed rationally in terms of $\Theta$. Then $\Omega(\Theta)$ is a divisor of $\Omega(\alpha)$, and its degree divides the degree of $\Omega(\alpha)$.

The field $\Omega(\alpha)$ is called primitive if it contains no imprimitive quantities apart from the quantities of $\Omega$; otherwise it is imprimitive. A field of prime degree is necessarily primitive. More generally, every divisor of $\Omega(\alpha)$ that contains $\Omega$ is an algebraic field $\Omega(\beta)$ over $\Omega$, its degree divides the degree of $\Omega(\alpha)$, and equal degrees give identical fields. Thus an algebraic field over $\Omega$ is primitive precisely when, apart from $\Omega$ and itself, it has no divisor field over $\Omega$.

\sect{145}{Normal Fields. Galois Resolvents}

If $\Omega(\alpha)$ is an algebraic field, then the conjugate fields
\[
        \Omega(\alpha),\Omega(\alpha_1),\ldots,\Omega(\alpha_{m-1})
\]
all have the same degree; hence if one is contained in another, the two are identical. A field identical with all its conjugate fields is called a normal field. Such fields are also called Galois fields.

If a field $\Omega(\rho)$ of degree $\mu$ has the property that all numbers $\rho_1,\rho_2,\ldots,\rho_{\mu-1}$ conjugate to $\rho$ are contained in $\Omega(\rho)$, then it is a normal field.

An equation is called a normal equation if it is irreducible and all its roots can be rationally expressed in $\Omega$ by means of any one of them. A primitive element of a normal field of degree $\mu$ is a root of a normal equation of degree $\mu$, and conversely the field generated by a root of a normal equation is a normal field of the same degree.

Every algebraic field can be led back to a normal field as follows. Let
\[
\tag{1}
        F(x)=0
\]
be any equation in $\Omega$ of degree $m$, reducible or irreducible, but without repeated roots, and let its roots be
\[
\tag{2}
        \alpha,\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_{m-1}.
\]
The field
\[
\tag{4}
        N=\Omega(\alpha,\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_{m-1})
\]
generated by all roots is called the Galois field of $F(x)=0$. If $F(x)$ is irreducible, $N$ is also called the norm of any of the conjugate fields $\Omega(\alpha_i)$.

Choose a primitive element $\rho$ of $N$ and write $N=\Omega(\rho)$. Let $\mu$ be the degree of $N$, and let
\[
\tag{5}
        g(t)=0
\]
be the irreducible equation of degree $\mu$ satisfied by $\rho$. Since $\rho$ is a rational function of the roots $\alpha,\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_{m-1}$, permuting these roots gives quantities $\rho,\rho',\rho'',\ldots$. The product
\[
        G(t)=(t-\rho)(t-\rho')(t-\rho'')\cdots
\]
has coefficients symmetric in the roots of $F(x)$ and therefore in $\Omega$. Since $G(t)$ and $g(t)$ have a common root and $g(t)$ is irreducible, $g(t)$ divides $G(t)$; hence all roots of $g(t)$ are contained in $N$. Thus $N$ is a normal field.

A Galois resolvent of $F(x)=0$ is an irreducible equation $g(t)=0$ such that all roots of $F$ can be rationally expressed by one root $\rho$ of $g$, and one root of $g$ can be rationally expressed by the roots of $F$. Every Galois resolvent is a normal equation, and every normal equation is its own Galois resolvent.

\sect{146}{The Substitutions of a Normal Field}

Let $\Omega(\rho)$ be a normal field of degree $\mu$ and $\rho$ a primitive element. Let
\[
\tag{1}
        g(t)=0
\]
be the irreducible equation of degree $\mu$ with one root $t=\rho$. The elements conjugate to $\rho$ are
\[
\tag{2}
        \rho,\rho_1,\rho_2,\ldots,\rho_{\mu-1}.
\]
Since all of them lie in the normal field, there are polynomials $\Theta_i(t)$ in $\Omega$, of degree at most $\mu-1$, such that
\[
\tag{3}
        \rho_1=\Theta_1(\rho),\quad
        \rho_2=\Theta_2(\rho),\quad\ldots,\quad
        \rho_{\mu-1}=\Theta_{\mu-1}(\rho).
\]
If $\omega$ is any element of $\Omega(\rho)$, its conjugates have the form
\[
\tag{4}
        \omega=\varphi(\rho),\quad
        \omega_1=\varphi(\rho_1),\ldots,\quad
        \omega_{\mu-1}=\varphi(\rho_{\mu-1}).
\]

Because $g(t)$ is irreducible, if a rational function $\Phi(t)$ in $\Omega$ has one root in common with $g(t)$, then
\[
        \Phi(\rho),\Phi(\rho_1),\ldots,\Phi(\rho_{\mu-1})
\]
all vanish.

Now replace $\rho$ in one of the functions $\Theta_k(t)$ by another root $\rho_h$. The result is again one of the roots. Indeed the set
\[
\tag{5}
        \rho_h,\Theta_1(\rho_h),\Theta_2(\rho_h),\ldots,\Theta_{\mu-1}(\rho_h)
\]
coincides, apart from order, with
\[
\tag{6}
        \rho,\Theta_1(\rho),\Theta_2(\rho),\ldots,\Theta_{\mu-1}(\rho).
\]
If two members of (5) were equal, then by the preceding principle the corresponding two members of (6) would be equal, which is impossible.

Thus, when $\rho$ is replaced by $\rho_h$, every quantity conjugate to $\rho$ passes to a definite other such quantity, and no two different quantities pass to the same one.

Replacing $\rho$ in all rational functions of $\rho$ by another root $\rho_a$ defines a substitution, denoted
\[
        \delta_a=(\rho,\rho_a),\qquad a=0,1,2,\ldots,\mu-1.
\]
The substitution $\delta_0$ is the identity. If $\rho_h=\Theta_h(\rho)$, then $\delta_a$ carries $\rho_h$ to
\[
        \rho_k=\Theta_h(\rho_a).
\]
Likewise an arbitrary element $\omega=\varphi(\rho)$ is carried to $\omega_a=\varphi(\rho_a)$. The same substitution may therefore be written
\[
\tag{9}
        \delta_a=(\rho_h,\rho_k)=(\rho_h,\Theta_h\Theta_a(\rho)).
\]
Here $\rho_h$ may be any root of $g(t)$, and the corresponding $\rho_k$ is then determined by $\delta_a$. Conversely either root in such a pair may be prescribed and the other is then determined.

Thus there are exactly $\mu$ distinct substitutions of $\Omega(\rho)$, including the identity. Each carries the totality of elements of the field into itself, every element going to a definite element and no two different elements going to the same one.

An element $\omega=\varphi(\rho)$ is said to allow or admit the substitution $(\rho,\rho_a)$, or $\delta_a$, if
\[
        \varphi(\rho)=\varphi(\rho_a).
\]
Elements admitting no substitution other than the identity are the primitive elements of the field. An element admitting all substitutions $\delta_a$ is necessarily an element of $\Omega$, for it is identical with all its conjugates and so satisfies an equation of first degree in $\Omega$.

\sect{147}{Composition of Substitutions}

If in a function of $\rho$ we first replace $\rho$ by $\rho_a$ and then $\rho_a$ by $\rho_b$, the result is the same as if $\rho$ had from the beginning been replaced by $\rho_b$. Thus if
\[
\tag{1}
        \delta=(\rho,\rho_a),\qquad
        \delta'=(\rho_a,\rho_b),\qquad
        \delta''=(\rho,\rho_b),
\]
then carrying out $\delta$ and then $\delta'$ has the same effect as carrying out $\delta''$ once. We therefore call $\delta''$ the composition of $\delta$ and $\delta'$ and write
\[
\tag{2}
        \delta\delta'=\delta''.
\]

Of the three substitutions connected by $\delta\delta'=\delta''$, any two may be given arbitrarily, and the third is then uniquely determined.

The notation is multiplicative, but the order of the factors is essential: in general $\delta\delta'$ and $\delta'\delta$ need not be the same. The commutative law need not hold, but the associative law does:
\[
\tag{3}
        (\delta\delta')\delta''=\delta(\delta'\delta'').
\]
Indeed choose roots so that
\[
\tag{4}
        \delta=(\rho,\rho_a),\qquad
        \delta'=(\rho_a,\rho_b),\qquad
        \delta''=(\rho_b,\rho_c).
\]
Then both $(\delta\delta')\delta''$ and $\delta(\delta'\delta'')$ carry $\rho$ to $\rho_c$. Hence both are $(\rho,\rho_c)$.

We therefore denote the composition of three substitutions by $\delta\delta'\delta''$, and similarly compose any number of adjacent components until the symbolic product has contracted to a single substitution. For example
\[
        (\rho,\rho_a)(\rho_a,\rho_b)(\rho_b,\rho_c)(\rho_c,\rho_e)
        =(\rho,\rho_e),
\]
and the same argument extends to any number of components.




\subsection*{End of \S\,147}

On the basis of the properties established in the preceding section, we call the totality of the $\mu$ substitutions of the normal field $\Omega(\rho)$ a group, namely a finite group of degree $\mu$. It is also called the group of substitutions of the field $\Omega(\rho)$. We shall deal with the general theory of groups in detail in later sections.

\sect{148}{Permutation groups}

In \S\,145 we saw how, for an equation
\[
        F(x)=0
\tag{1}
\]
of degree $m$ in $\Omega$, assuming only that it has no repeated roots, one can determine a normal field $\Omega(\rho)$ containing all its roots. If these roots are denoted by
\[
        \alpha,\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,
\alpha_{m-1},
\tag{2}
\]
then
\[
        \alpha=\chi(\rho),\qquad
        \alpha_1=\chi_1(\rho),\qquad \ldots,
        \alpha_{m-1}=\chi_{m-1}(\rho),
\tag{3}
\]
where the $\chi$ are rational functions in $\Omega$. If in place of $\rho$ one puts a conjugate value $\rho_1$, that is, if one performs a substitution $\delta=(\rho,\rho_1)$, then each root is changed into a definite other root, and two different roots are never changed into the same one. Indeed, from $F(\alpha)=F(\chi(\rho))=0$ it follows by \S\,146 that $\chi(\rho_1)$ is again a root; and the uniqueness follows from the general fact already proved that no two different elements of $\Omega(\rho)$ can be sent by one substitution to the same element.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item A substitution $\delta$ therefore produces among the roots $\alpha$ a certain permutation: each of the indices $0,1,2,\ldots,m-1$ is replaced by a definite other index.
\end{enumerate}

This leads us to study in general the permutations of the $m$ symbols
\[
        0,1,2,
\ldots,m-1.
\]
If $0$ goes to $a_0$, $1$ to $a_1$, and so on, then
\[
        a_0,a_1,a_2,
\ldots,a_{m-1}
\tag{4}
\]
is another arrangement of the symbols $0,1,2,
\ldots,m-1$. We write this transition as
\[
        \pi_a=\perm{0&1&2&\cdots&m-1}{a_0&a_1&a_2&\cdots&a_{m-1}}
\tag{5}
\]
and call it a permutation. The number of all possible permutations of $m$ elements is
\[
        n(m)=1\cdot2\cdot3\cdots m=m!,
\]
including the identity permutation
\[
        \pi_0=\perm{0&1&2&\cdots&m-1}{0&1&2&\cdots&m-1}.
\]

The notation (5) is unchanged if its columns are rearranged. Thus, for another ordering $b_0,b_1,
\ldots,b_{m-1}$ of the symbols, the same permutation may be written
\[
        \pi_a=\perm{b_0&b_1&\cdots&b_{m-1}}{b_0\pi_a&b_1\pi_a&\cdots&b_{m-1}\pi_a}.
\tag{6}
\]

From this one obtains the notion of composition. If an index $a$ is first replaced by $b$ and then $b$ by $c$, the result is the same as replacing $a$ at once by $c$. For
\[
        \pi_a=\perm{0&1&\cdots&m-1}{a_0&a_1&\cdots&a_{m-1}},
        \qquad
        \pi_b=\perm{0&1&\cdots&m-1}{b_0&b_1&\cdots&b_{m-1}},
\]
the successive performance first of $\pi_a$ and then of $\pi_b$ gives
\[
        \pi_a\pi_b=\perm{0&1&\cdots&m-1}{b_{a_0}&b_{a_1}&\cdots&b_{a_{m-1}}}.
\tag{7}
\]
In general $\pi_a\pi_b$ is not equal to $\pi_b\pi_a$. The associative law does hold:
\[
        (\pi_a\pi_b)\pi_c=\pi_a(\pi_b\pi_c).
\tag{8}
\]
For if an index $p$ is sent by $\pi_a$ to $q$, by $\pi_b$ to $r$, and by $\pi_c$ to $s$, then both sides send $p$ to $s$.

Every permutation $\pi_a$ has a definite inverse $\pi_a^{-1}$, which undoes it. If $\pi_a$ has the form (5), then
\[
        \pi_a^{-1}=\perm{a_0&a_1&\cdots&a_{m-1}}{0&1&\cdots&m-1}.
\tag{9}
\]
It satisfies
\[
        \pi_a\pi_a^{-1}=\pi_a^{-1}\pi_a=\pi_0,
\tag{10}
\]
and composition with the identity changes nothing:
\[
        \pi_0\pi_a=\pi_a\pi_0=\pi_a.
\tag{11}
\]
If
\[
        \pi_a\pi_b=\pi_c,
\tag{12}
\]
then any two of the three permutations determine the third uniquely:
\[
        \pi_a=\pi_c\pi_b^{-1},\qquad
        \pi_b=\pi_a^{-1}\pi_c.
\tag{13}
\]

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=3]
\item A system
\[
        Q=\{\pi_0,\pi_1,
\ldots,\pi_{q-1}\}
\]
selected from all $n(m)$ permutations of $m$ elements is called a permutation group if it is closed under composition: the composition of any two of its permutations again belongs to the same system. The degree of the group is the number of its permutations.
\end{enumerate}

All $n(m)$ permutations form a group, the symmetric group. The single identity permutation also forms a group.

\sect{149}{The Galois group}

When the roots of $F(x)=0$ are represented as in (3) by elements of the normal field $\Omega(\rho)$, each substitution of the normal field gives a permutation of the indices of the roots. Different substitutions give different permutations. For if
\[
        \rho=\theta(\alpha,\alpha_1,
\ldots,
\alpha_{m-1}),
\tag{1}
\]
then for a conjugate value
\[
        \rho_k=\theta(\chi(\rho_k),\chi_1(\rho_k),\ldots,\chi_{m-1}(\rho_k)).
\tag{2}
\]
If two substitutions produced the same permutation of the roots, the corresponding values of $\rho_k$ would be equal.

We say that the permutation $\pi_h$ corresponds to the substitution $\delta_h$ when $\delta_h$ produces $\pi_h$ among the roots. The identity substitution corresponds to the identity permutation, and if $\delta_h,\delta_k$ correspond to $\pi_h,\pi_k$, then $\delta_h\delta_k$ corresponds to $\pi_h\pi_k$.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item To the $\mu$ substitutions
\[
        \delta,\delta_1,
\delta_2,
\ldots,
\delta_{
\mu-1}
\tag{3}
\]
of $\Omega(\rho)$ there correspond $\mu$ permutations
\[
        \pi,\pi_1,
\pi_2,
\ldots,
\pi_{
\mu-1}
\tag{4}
\]
of the $m$ symbols, in such a way that composition of substitutions corresponds to composition of permutations.
\end{enumerate}

The permutations (4) therefore form a group $P$. We call it the Galois group of the equation $F(x)=0$, or of any one of the fields $\Omega(\alpha),\Omega(\alpha_1),\ldots$. It is isomorphic to the substitution group of the normal field.

The characteristic properties of the Galois group are the following reformulations of \S\,146.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\alph*)]
\item Every rational equation over $\Omega$ holding among the $m$ roots remains true after applying any permutation of the Galois group.
\item Every rational function over $\Omega$ of the $m$ roots which is left fixed by all permutations of the Galois group is an element of $\Omega$.
\item If a permutation of the roots can be applied to every rational equation over $\Omega$ holding among the roots, then that permutation belongs to the Galois group. Hence the Galois group is the totality of all permutations applicable to all rational equations among the roots.
\item If a group $P$ of permutations of the roots has the properties a) and b), then $P$ is the Galois group of the equation.
\end{enumerate}

For d), let $P$ have $\nu$ permutations. Applying these permutations to a primitive element $\rho$ gives $\rho_1,
\rho_2,
\ldots,
\rho_
\nu$. The product
\[
        g'(t)=(t-\rho_1)(t-\rho_2)\cdots(t-\rho_
\nu)
\]
is invariant under $P$ and is therefore in $\Omega[t]$. It is also a factor of the irreducible Galois resolvent $g(t)$; hence it is equal to it. Thus $\nu$ is not smaller than the degree of the Galois group, and $P$ is that group.

If $\rho$ is chosen as a rational function of the roots so that all $n(m)$ values obtained by permuting the roots are distinct, then
\[
        G(t)=(t-\rho)(t-\rho')(t-\rho'')\cdots
\]
is a function over $\Omega$. Each of its roots is a primitive element of the normal field $N=\Omega(\alpha,
\alpha_1,
\ldots,
\alpha_{m-1})$. Thus $G(t)$ splits into irreducible factors of degree $\mu$, and $\mu$ divides $n(m)$. The degree of the Galois group therefore divides $m!$.

If the Galois resolvent of an equation of degree $m$ has the maximal degree $n(m)$, then, in Kronecker's terminology, the equation has no affect. The smaller the degree $\mu$ of the Galois resolvent, the higher the affect. The quotient
\[
        n(m):\mu
\]
is called the degree of the affect. It is $1$ for an equation without affect; if it reaches $n(m)$, the roots already lie in the rationality domain and the equation is solved.

In Galois's conception, solving $F(x)=0$ means adjoining algebraic quantities of the simplest possible kind so as gradually to diminish the group, or equivalently to raise the affect, until the maximal degree is reached.

The general equation of degree $m$ has no affect over the field of rational functions of its coefficients. Indeed, the coefficients are the elementary symmetric functions of the roots; every permutation of the roots leaves them unchanged. If a rational irreducible factor of the general resolvent vanishes at one root of the resolvent, then after arbitrary permutation of the roots it vanishes at all roots of the resolvent, and is therefore the whole resolvent.

\sect{150}{Transitive and intransitive groups}

The Galois group gives a simple criterion for reducibility. Suppose $F(x)$ is reducible and $f(x)$ is a factor of degree $n<m$ with roots
\[
        \alpha,
\alpha_1,
\ldots,
\alpha_{n-1},
\tag{1}
\]
the remaining roots being
\[
        \alpha_n,
\alpha_{n+1},
\ldots,
\alpha_{m-1}.
\tag{2}
\]
No permutation of the Galois group can send a root in (1) to a root in (2), since $f(\alpha')=0$ would imply $f(\alpha'')=0$ after an admissible permutation.

Conversely, if the group only permutes a proper subset of the roots among itself, then the product
\[
        (x-\alpha)(x-\alpha_1)\cdots(x-\alpha_{n-1})
\]
is invariant under the group and hence has coefficients in $\Omega$; thus $F(x)$ is reducible.

A permutation group is called transitive if for any two elements it contains a permutation sending the first to the second. Otherwise it is intransitive; the elements then break into systems of intransitivity that are not mixed by the group. Therefore:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item The equation $F(x)=0$ is reducible or irreducible according as its Galois group is intransitive or transitive.
\end{enumerate}

\sect{151}{Primitive and imprimitive groups}

Let $f(x)=0$ be an irreducible equation of degree $n$ with transitive Galois group $P$ and roots
\[
        \alpha,
\alpha_1,
\ldots,
\alpha_{n-1}.
\tag{1}
\]
If the field $\Omega(\alpha)$ is imprimitive and $\vartheta=\chi(\alpha)$ is an imprimitive element whose conjugates split into $s$ systems of $r$ equal values, so that $n=rs$, $r>1$, $s<n$, then the roots may be arranged in rows
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
        A&=&\alpha,
\alpha_1,
\ldots,
\alpha_{r-1},\\
        B&=&\beta,
\beta_1,
\ldots,
\beta_{r-1},\\
        &\vdots&\\
        S&=&\delta,
\delta_1,
\ldots,
\delta_{r-1}
\end{array}
\tag{2}
\]
so that
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
        \vartheta&=&\chi(\alpha)=\chi(\alpha_1)=\cdots=\chi(\alpha_{r-1}),\\
        \vartheta_1&=&\chi(\beta)=\chi(\beta_1)=\cdots=\chi(\beta_{r-1}),\\
        &\vdots&
\end{array}
\tag{3}
\]
are the conjugate values of $\vartheta$. By \S\,144,
\[
        (t-\vartheta)(t-\vartheta_1)\cdots(t-\vartheta_{s-1})=0
\tag{4}
\]
is irreducible over $\Omega$.

It follows from (3) that the permutations of the group must send the elements of a row into elements of one row as a whole: two elements of one row can never be sent to elements of different rows. Groups with this property are called imprimitive, and the rows are called systems of imprimitivity.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item If $\Omega(\alpha)$ is imprimitive, then $P$ is an imprimitive group.
\end{enumerate}

Conversely, assume $P$ is imprimitive with systems (2). Choose a symmetric function of the elements of a row so that its values
\[
        y,
\quad y_1,
\quad \ldots,
\quad y_{s-1}
\tag{8}
\]
for the different rows are distinct. For instance one may use the polynomials
\[
        \psi(t,A)=(t-\alpha)(t-\alpha_1)\cdots(t-\alpha_{r-1}),
\tag{9}
\]
and the corresponding expressions for the other rows, after a suitable rational specialization of $t$. Then
\[
        \varphi(u)=(u-y)(u-y_1)\cdots(u-y_{s-1})
\tag{10}
\]
has coefficients in $\Omega$ and is irreducible. Every symmetric function of the roots in one row is rationally expressible in $y$, and in particular
\[
        \psi(t,A)=\psi(t,y)
\tag{11}
\]
is an irreducible equation of degree $r$ over $\Omega(y)$ whose roots are the elements of $A$.

Moreover $y$ is rationally expressible by $\alpha$. Since
\[
        \psi(\alpha,y)=0,
\tag{12}
\]
and $\psi(\alpha,y_i)\ne0$ for $i>0$, the equations
\[
        \psi(\alpha,u)=0,
        \qquad
        \varphi(u)=0
\tag{13}
\]
have exactly one common root $u=y$; their greatest common divisor is linear and gives $y$ rationally in terms of $\alpha$. Hence:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=3]
\item A primitive field has a primitive group.
\end{enumerate}

\sect{152}{Action of permutation groups on functions of independent variables}

We now begin a closer study of permutation groups. Let
\[
        u,u_1,u_2,
\ldots,u_{m-1}
\tag{1}
\]
be independent symbols, and let
\[
        \psi(u,u_1,u_2,
\ldots,u_{m-1})
\tag{2}
\]
be a rational integral function with coefficients in some field. Two such functions are regarded as equal only when their expressions, ordered by powers and products of the $u$, have equal corresponding coefficients.

If a permutation $\pi$ is applied to the indices, the function $\psi$ may be unchanged. We then say that $\psi$ admits $\pi$. If it admits all permutations, it is symmetric.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item The permutations admitted by a given function $\psi$ form a group.
\end{enumerate}
For if $\psi$ admits both $\pi$ and $\pi'$, then it admits $\pi\pi'$, and with $\pi$ it admits $\pi^{-1}$.

If $P$ is a permutation group, a function is said to belong to $P$ if it admits all permutations of $P$ and changes under every permutation not belonging to $P$. Such functions exist for every group. Take a function $\rho$ of the variables which assumes $n(m)$ distinct values under all possible permutations, and consider its values under $P$:
\[
        \rho,
\rho_1,
\ldots,
\rho_{
\mu-1}.
\tag{1}
\]
Then
\[
        \Phi(t)=(t-\rho)(t-\rho_1)\cdots(t-\rho_{
\mu-1})
\tag{2}
\]
is invariant under $P$. A permutation outside $P$ gives a different product; choosing a suitable rational value of $t$ gives a function belonging to $P$.

The functions belonging to the group of all permutations are the symmetric functions; hence that group is the symmetric group. A primitive function belonging to the identity group gives an unambiguous notation for permutations: if $\rho'$ is obtained from $\rho$, the corresponding permutation may be denoted
\[
        \pi'=(\rho,\rho').
\]

\sect{153}{Decomposition of permutations into transpositions and cycles}

A transposition is the interchange of two symbols, for example $(0,1)$. It is its own inverse. If
\[
        \pi=\perm{0&1&2&\cdots&m-1}{a_0&a_1&a_2&\cdots&a_{m-1}},
\]
then $\pi(m-1,a_{m-1})$ fixes $m-1$ and is a permutation of at most $m-1$ symbols. By induction:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item Every permutation can be decomposed into transpositions, in infinitely many ways.
\item If a permutation group on $m$ symbols contains all transpositions with one fixed symbol, say $(0,1),(0,2),\ldots,(0,m-1)$, then it is the whole symmetric group. For $(1,2)=(0,1)(0,2)(0,1)$, and similarly for all other transpositions.
\end{enumerate}

A permutation is cyclic if its symbols can be ordered so that each is sent to the next and the last to the first:
\[
        (0,1,2,
\ldots,m-1).
\]
The starting point is immaterial. Every permutation decomposes uniquely into disjoint cycles, if cycles of length one are counted. Disjoint cycles commute.

Compare the decompositions into cycles and into transpositions. If $\pi$ is composed with a transposition $\tau$, the number of cycles either increases or decreases by one: a transposition splits one cycle if its two symbols lie in that cycle, and joins two cycles if its symbols lie in different cycles. If $\pi$ has $\nu$ cycles on $m$ symbols and is written as a product of $\mu$ transpositions, then
\[
        \mu\equiv m-\nu \pmod 2.
\]
Thus the parity of $\mu$ depends only on $\pi$.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=4]
\item The permutations of $m$ symbols fall into two classes: the first can be decomposed into an even number, the second into an odd number, of transpositions.
\end{enumerate}

The two classes have equal size, $\frac12 n(m)$. The composition of two permutations of the same class is of the first class; the composition of one of each class is of the second. Hence:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=5]
\item The permutations of the first class form a group.
\end{enumerate}

A function that changes sign when two variables are interchanged, such as
\[
        \prod_{0\le i<j\le m-1}(u_i-u_j),
\]
is called alternating. Permutations of the first class leave an alternating function unchanged; permutations of the second class change its sign. The group of the first class is therefore called the alternating group.

A cycle of length $n$ can be written as
\[
        (1,2,3,
\ldots,n)=(1,2)(1,3)\cdots(1,n),
\]
and is therefore even if $n$ is odd, odd if $n$ is even.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=6]
\item Every even permutation can be decomposed into three-cycles.
\item A permutation group containing all three-cycles with two fixed symbols contains the whole alternating group.
\item If $m>4$, a permutation group that contains all products of two disjoint transpositions, such as $(0,1)(2,3)$, is divisible by the alternating group.
\end{enumerate}

The proofs use, for example,
\[
        (0,1)(0,2)=(0,1,2),
        \qquad
        (0,3)(1,2)=(0,1,2)(0,1,3),
\]
and, for $m>4$,
\[
        (0,1,2)=(0,1)(3,4)(3,4)(0,2).
\]

For transitive groups one obtains:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=9]
\item If a transitive permutation group of $m$ symbols contains a single transposition, then it is either the symmetric group or it is imprimitive.
\item If a transitive permutation group of $m$ symbols contains a three-cycle, then it contains the whole alternating group or it is imprimitive.
\item In the imprimitive case, the group contains in each system of imprimitivity the whole symmetric group in case 9, and the whole alternating group in case 10.
\end{enumerate}

Finally, repeated composition of a permutation is denoted by powers. For a permutation $\pi$ there is a least positive exponent $e$ such that
\[
        \pi^e=1.
\]
Then
\[
        1,\pi,\pi^2,
\ldots,
\pi^{e-1}
\]
are distinct and repeat periodically. This sequence is the period of $\pi$, and $e$ is the degree of $\pi$. The period forms a group.

If $\pi=(0,1,2,
\ldots,n-1)$, then $\pi$ has degree $n$. If $\pi$ is decomposed into several cycles, the degree of $\pi$ is the least common multiple of their lengths. For example
\[
        \pi=(0,1)(2,3,4)
\]
has degree $6$, since
\[
\begin{aligned}
        \pi^2&=(0)(1)(2,4,3),\\
        \pi^3&=(0,1)(2)(3)(4),\\
        \pi^4&=(0)(1)(2,3,4),\\
        \pi^5&=(0,1)(2,4,3),\\
        \pi^6&=1.
\end{aligned}
\]
If $\pi$ occurs in a permutation group $P$, then $P$ contains the whole period of $\pi$.


\sect{154}{Divisors of groups. Cosets and conjugate groups}

In \S\,152 we saw that a function of $m$ independent variables always determines a permutation group on $m$ symbols. This need not remain true when definite quantities are put in place of the independent variables. Galois theory, however, removes this difference, or at least reduces it to its essential core. Let $F(x)=0$ be an equation of degree $m$ in a field $\Omega$, with distinct roots
\[
        \alpha,\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\ldots,
\alpha_{m-1}.
\]
We denote the normal field
\[
        \Omega(\rho)=\Omega(\alpha,
\alpha_1,
\ldots,
\alpha_{m-1})
\]
by $N$. By the Galois group $P$ of the field $N$, or of the equation $F(x)=0$, we may mean either the substitution group of $N$ or the isomorphic permutation group on the indices of the roots.

Let $p$ be the degree of $P$, and write its operations as
\[
        P=\pi,\pi_1,
\pi_2,
\ldots,
\pi_{p-1}.
\tag{1}
\]
If a system $Q$ of operations contained in $P$,
\[
        Q=\chi,
\chi_1,
\chi_2,
\ldots,
\chi_{q-1},
\tag{2}
\]
is itself a group, then $Q$ is called a divisor, or subgroup, of $P$.

If $q<p$, choose an element $\pi_1$ of $P$ not in $Q$ and form
\[
        Q\pi_1=\chi\pi_1,
\chi_1\pi_1,
\ldots,
\chi_{q-1}\pi_1.
\tag{3}
\]
The elements of this system are mutually distinct and are distinct from those of $Q$. If $Q\pi_1$ does not yet exhaust $P$, choose $\pi_2$ outside $Q$ and $Q\pi_1$, and proceed. One obtains a decomposition
\[
        P=Q+Q\pi_1+Q\pi_2+\cdots+Q\pi_{j-1}.
\tag{4}
\]
The systems $Q,Q\pi_1,
\ldots,Q\pi_{j-1}$ are the cosets of $Q$ in $P$.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item The degree $p$ of a group is divisible by the degree $q$ of each of its divisors. If $j$ is the number of cosets, then
\[
        p=jq.
\tag{5}
\]
The quotient $j=p/q$ is the index of $Q$ in $P$.
\item If any operation $\pi$ of $P$ is applied to the system of cosets, the same system is obtained again, only in a different order.
\end{enumerate}

One may also consider cosets of the form $\pi Q$; for the algebraic application the form (4) is more convenient.

An element $\psi$ of the field $N$ is said to belong to the group $Q$ if it is fixed by all operations of $Q$ and changed by every other operation of $P$. This belonging is understood relative to $P$; if $P$ is the symmetric group, it coincides with the earlier definition of \S\,152.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=3]
\item To every divisor $Q$ of $P$ there belong elements of $N$, and every element of $N$ belongs to a definite divisor of $P$.
\end{enumerate}

The first assertion was proved in \S\,152. For the second, if $\pi$ is applied to $\psi$, write the resulting value as $\psi(\pi)$. Then
\[
        \psi(\pi)(\pi')=\psi(\pi\pi').
\tag{6}
\]
The operations that leave $\psi$ fixed therefore form a group.

If $\psi$ belongs to $Q$ and $\pi_i$ is not in $Q$, then all operations in the coset $Q\pi_i$ carry $\psi$ to one and the same value, different from $\psi$; and conversely only the operations of that coset do so. Thus:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=4]
\item A function $\psi$ belonging to $Q$ is transformed by all operations of a coset $Q\pi_i$, and by no other operation of $P$, into one definite value $\psi_i$ different from $\psi$.
\item The values
\[
        \psi,
\psi_1,
\psi_2,
\ldots,
\psi_{j-1}
\tag{10}
\]
are permuted when one and the same operation of $P$ is applied to all of them.
\end{enumerate}

These values are the conjugate functions of $\psi$. The group to which $\psi_i=\psi(\pi_i)$ belongs is
\[
        \pi_i^{-1}Q\pi_i.
\tag{11}
\]
The groups
\[
        Q,
\quad \pi_1^{-1}Q\pi_1,
\quad \pi_2^{-1}Q\pi_2,
\ldots,
\pi_{j-1}^{-1}Q\pi_{j-1}
\tag{12}
\]
are called conjugate divisors, or conjugate groups. The derivation of $\pi^{-1}Q\pi$ from $Q$ is a transformation of $Q$ by $\pi$.

For computing transformed permutations one uses the rule:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=6]
\item One obtains $\pi^{-1}\chi\pi$ by applying the permutation $\pi$ to the letters occurring in the cycles of $\chi$.
\end{enumerate}
Thus if
\[
        \chi=(a,b,c,
\ldots)(a',b',c',
\ldots)\cdots,
\]
then
\[
        \pi^{-1}\chi\pi=(a\pi,b\pi,c\pi,
\ldots)(a'\pi,b'\pi,c'\pi,
\ldots)\cdots.
\]

The coset decomposition also proves:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=7]
\item The degree of a transitive permutation group on $m$ symbols is always a multiple of $m$.
\end{enumerate}
Indeed, the operations fixing one chosen symbol form a divisor $Q$, and the cosets correspond to the possible images of that symbol.

\sect{155}{Reduction of the Galois resolvent by adjunction. Normal divisors}

Let $P$ be the Galois group of degree $p$, let $Q$ be a divisor of degree $q$ and index $j$, and let $\psi$ be a function belonging to $Q$. Let its conjugate values be
\[
        \psi,
\psi_1,
\psi_2,
\ldots,
\psi_{j-1}.
\tag{1}
\]

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item The values (1) are the roots of an irreducible equation of degree $j$ over $\Omega$.
\end{enumerate}
For
\[
        \varphi(t)=(t-\psi)(t-\psi_1)\cdots(t-\psi_{j-1})
\tag{2}
\]
is invariant under all operations of $P$, and hence has coefficients in $\Omega$. If a polynomial over $\Omega$ vanishes at $\psi$, then applying all operations of $P$ shows that it vanishes at all the conjugates; this gives irreducibility.

The theorem of Lagrange follows:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=2]
\item Every element of $N$ fixed by all permutations of $Q$ lies in the field $\Omega(\psi)$, provided $\psi$ belongs to $Q$.
\end{enumerate}

Let $\omega$ be such an element. Its values corresponding to the conjugates of $\psi$ are
\[
        \omega,
\omega_1,
\omega_2,
\ldots,
\omega_{j-1},
\tag{4}
\]
not necessarily all distinct. The sum
\[
        \sum_{i=0}^{j-1}
        \frac{\omega_i}{t-\psi_i}
\]
is invariant under $P$, hence a function in $\Omega(t)$. Substituting $t=\psi$ expresses $\omega$ rationally in $\psi$.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=3]
\item If one adjoins to $\Omega$ a function $\psi$ belonging to $Q$, then the Galois group is reduced from $P$ to $Q$.
\end{enumerate}
Indeed, the quantities in $\Omega'=\Omega(\psi)$ are precisely those left fixed by all operations of $Q$; by \S\,149 these characterize the Galois group over the new field.

The Galois resolvent $g(t)$ decomposes accordingly. If the operations of $Q$ carry a root of the resolvent through
\[
        \rho,
\rho_1,
\ldots,
\rho_{q-1},
\]
and the operations of $Q\pi_i$ through
\[
        \rho_{0,i},
\rho_{1,i},
\ldots,
\rho_{q-1,i},
\]
then
\[
        g_i(t)=(t-\rho_{0,i})(t-\rho_{1,i})
\cdots(t-\rho_{q-1,i})
\]
has coefficients in $\Omega(\psi)$, and
\[
        g(t)=g_0(t)g_1(t)\cdots g_{j-1}(t),
\tag{6}
\]
with all factors of degree $q$.

If $Q,Q',Q'',\ldots$ are divisors of $P$, their intersection $R$ is their greatest common divisor. If $\psi$ belongs to $Q$ and $\psi'$ to $Q'$, the coefficients $x,x'$ may be chosen so that $x\psi+x'\psi'$ belongs to $R$. Therefore:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=4]
\item If functions belonging to $Q,Q',Q'',\ldots$ are adjoined, the group is reduced to the intersection of those divisors.
\end{enumerate}

A divisor $Q$ of $P$ is called normal if
\[
        \pi^{-1}Q\pi=Q
\]
for every operation $\pi$ of $P$.

If $Q$ is normal, adjoining a single root of (2) is equivalent to adjoining all its roots. If $Q$ is not normal, then the intersection $R$ of all conjugate divisors $\pi^{-1}Q\pi$ is a normal divisor of $P$.

A group with no normal divisors except itself and the identity group is called simple. If $Q$ is normal in $P$ and $R$ is normal in $Q$, $R$ need not be normal in $P$; but if $R$ is normal in $P$, it is normal in every divisor of $P$ that contains it.

\sect{156}{The group of resolvents}

The auxiliary equations
\[
        \varphi(t)=0
\]
for functions belonging to divisors $Q$ will be called resolvents in a general sense. If $Q$ is the identity group, then by Lagrange every element of the normal field, in particular the roots of the original equation, is rationally expressible in $\psi$; the resolvent is then a Galois resolvent.

There are two cases.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item If the conjugate groups $\pi^{-1}Q\pi$ have only the identity group in common, then adjoining all roots of the resolvent is equivalent to adjoining a function belonging to the identity group. The resolvent is a total resolvent.
\item If the conjugate groups have a common divisor $R$ different from the identity and of degree $r$, then adjoining all conjugate functions only decomposes the Galois resolvent into factors of degree $r$. The resolvent is a partial resolvent.
\end{enumerate}

If $P$ is simple, only total resolvents occur; when $P$ has normal divisors, each normal divisor gives rise to a partial resolvent.

The Galois group of the resolvent $\varphi(t)=0$ consists of the permutations of
\[
        \psi,
\psi_1,
\psi_2,
\ldots,
\psi_{j-1}
\tag{1}
\]
that are induced by operations of $P$. Two operations $\pi,\pi'$ induce the same permutation of (1) exactly when $\pi'\pi^{-1}$ lies in the intersection $R$ of the conjugate groups. Thus the operations of one coset $R\pi$, and only these, induce the same permutation. Therefore the degree of the Galois group of the resolvent is
\[
        p:r,
\]
the index of $R$ in $P$. For $R=1$ the resolvent is total and its Galois group has degree $p$.

\sect{157}{Adjunction of arbitrary irrationalities}

We now consider an arbitrary adjunction. Let $\eta$ be a quantity satisfying an irreducible equation over $\Omega$, but not necessarily a function of the roots of the equation under consideration. If $\eta$ is adjoined to $\Omega$, the Galois group of $F(x)=0$ may become smaller. To reduce this to the preceding theory, form the common normal field containing both the normal field $N$ of the equation and the conjugates of $\eta$. In that enlarged normal field, adjoining $\eta$ corresponds to retaining the substitutions that fix $\eta$.

Thus, as far as the given equation is concerned, the effect of an arbitrary adjunction is measured by the intersection of the original Galois group with the divisor fixing the adjoined quantity. In particular, adjoining all roots of a resolvent gives a normal divisor, namely the intersection of the conjugate divisors belonging to the several roots. This is the group-theoretic reason for the distinction between total and partial resolvents.

\sect{158}{Reduction of imprimitive equations}

Let $f(x)=0$ be irreducible with imprimitive Galois group $P$. Arrange its roots in systems of equal length,
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
        A&=&\alpha,
\alpha_1,
\ldots,
\alpha_{r-1},\\
        B&=&\beta,
\beta_1,
\ldots,
\beta_{r-1},\\
        &\vdots&\\
        S&=&\delta,
\delta_1,
\ldots,
\delta_{r-1}.
\end{array}
\]
Let $\psi$ belong to the divisor $Q$ that keeps the row $A$ within itself. Then, after adjoining a quantity $y$ belonging to the systems, $f(x)$ decomposes into factors
\[
        f(x,y_A),
\quad f(x,y_B),
\quad \ldots,
\quad f(x,y_S),
\]
whose roots are the corresponding rows. These factors are irreducible over $\Omega(y)$.

The groups of the individual factors can be made identical by a suitable notation for the roots. Choose, for each row, a permutation carrying $A$ to that row, and name the roots accordingly. Then the operations occurring in $Q$ induce in every row the same permutations of the indices. Hence:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item In an irreducible imprimitive equation the roots in the separate rows may be denoted so that the partial equations
\[
        f(x,y_A)=0,
\quad f(x,y_B)=0,
\quad \ldots
\]
all have the same group over $\Omega(y)$.
\end{enumerate}
Without this choice of notation the groups are at least isomorphic.

Conversely, let a transitive group $P$ have an intransitive normal divisor $Q$. Let $A,B,
\ldots$ be the systems of intransitivity of $Q$. Since $Q$ is normal, the operations of $P$ send these systems into entire such systems. Thus they are systems of imprimitivity of $P$.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em,start=2]
\item A transitive group has a nontrivial intransitive normal divisor only when it is imprimitive. The systems of intransitivity of the normal divisor are systems of imprimitivity of the given group.
\item If an irreducible equation $f(x)=0$ becomes reducible after adjoining all roots of a resolvent, or one root of a normal equation of degree $j$, then it splits into several irreducible factors of equal degree and equal group. The number of these factors divides $j$, and is equal to $j$ when $j$ is prime.
\end{enumerate}

Consequently, if an irreducible primitive equation is reduced by adjoining the roots of a resolvent, it is completely solved. This always applies to equations of prime degree, since such equations cannot be imprimitive.

\section*{Fifteenth Section. Cyclic equations}

\sect{159}{Cubic equations}

From the standpoint now reached, we first consider equations of the third and fourth degrees. The permutations of three symbols consist of six elements: the identity, two three-cycles, and three transpositions,
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
        1 & (0,1,2) & (0,2,1)\\[0.25em]
        (1,2) & (2,0) & (0,1).
\end{array}
\tag{1}
\]
The first three
\[
        1,
\quad (0,1,2),
\quad (0,2,1)
\]
form the alternating group; they are the powers of the cyclic permutation $\pi=(0,1,2)$:
\[
        \pi^0=1,
\quad \pi,
\quad \pi^2.
\tag{2}
\]
For this reason it is called the cyclic group.

Let $\alpha,
\alpha_1,
\alpha_2$ be the roots of the cubic
\[
        f(x)=x^3-ax^2+bx-c=0.
\tag{3}
\]
Over the field of rational functions in the independent coefficients $a,b,c$, the group is (1). If one adjoins
\[
        \sqrt D=(\alpha-\alpha_1)(\alpha-\alpha_2)(\alpha_1-\alpha_2),
\tag{4}
\]
where
\[
        D=a^2b^2+18abc-4a^3c-4b^3-27c^2
\tag{5}
\]
is the discriminant, then the group is reduced to the cyclic group (2).

In the enlarged field every root is rationally expressible in every other root. The cubic equation is therefore, after adjoining $\sqrt D$, its own Galois resolvent. The same remains true over any special rationality domain $\Omega$ containing $a,b,c$ and $\sqrt D$, provided $f(x)$ is not already reducible there.

For the actual solution one seeks a function $v$ of the roots such that $v^3$ is fixed by the cyclic permutation. This requires adjoining the third roots of unity, or equivalently $\sqrt{-3}$. Let $\varepsilon$ be a primitive third root of unity and put
\[
        v=\alpha+\varepsilon\alpha_1+\varepsilon^2\alpha_2,
        \qquad
        v'=\alpha+\varepsilon^2\alpha_1+\varepsilon\alpha_2.
\tag{7}
\]
Under $\pi$, $v$ is changed to $\varepsilon^2v$ and $v'$ to $\varepsilon v'$, while $v^3$, $v'^3$, and $vv'$ remain fixed. With
\[
        A=\alpha^2\alpha_1+
\alpha_1^2\alpha_2+
\alpha_2^2\alpha,
        \qquad
        A'=\alpha_1^2\alpha+
\alpha_2^2\alpha_1+
\alpha^2\alpha_2,
\]
one has
\[
        A+A'=ab-3c,
\qquad
        A-A'=\sqrt D.
\]
Thus
\[
        v^3=a^3-\frac{9}{2}ab+\frac{27}{2}c+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{-3D},
\tag{8}
\]
and
\[
        v'^3=a^3-\frac{9}{2}ab+\frac{27}{2}c-\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{-3D}.
\tag{9}
\]
Furthermore
\[
        vv'=a^2-3b.
\tag{10}
\]
These formulas agree with
\[
        27D=4(a^2-3b)^3-(2a^3-9ab+27c)^2.
\]
Together with $a=\alpha+
\alpha_1+
\alpha_2$ and (7), they give Cardano's expressions
\[
\begin{aligned}
        3\alpha&=a+v+v',\\
        3\alpha_1&=a+\varepsilon^2v+\varepsilon v',\\
        3\alpha_2&=a+\varepsilon v+\varepsilon^2v'.
\end{aligned}
\]

\sect{160}{Permutation groups of four elements}

From four symbols $0,1,2,3$ one obtains $24$ permutations. The Galois group of the general quartic over the field of rational functions of the coefficients therefore has degree $24$. We list the permutations in cycle notation, with $1$ denoting the identity:
\[
\begin{array}{llll}
P=&1,&(0,1),&(0,2),\ (0,3),\ (1,2),\ (1,3),\ (2,3),\\
  &(0,1)(2,3),&(0,2)(1,3),&(0,3)(1,2),\\
  &(0,1,2),&(0,1,3),&(0,2,3),\ (1,2,3),\\
  &(0,2,1),&(0,3,1),&(0,3,2),\ (1,3,2),\\
  &(0,1,2,3),&(0,1,3,2),&(0,2,3,1),\\
  &(0,2,1,3),&(0,3,1,2),&(0,3,2,1).
\end{array}
\tag{1}
\]
Thus, besides the identity, there are six transpositions, eight three-cycles, six four-cycles, and three products of two transpositions.

The alternating group of degree $12$ is a normal divisor of $P$:
\[
\begin{array}{llll}
Q=&1,&(0,1)(2,3),&(0,2)(1,3),\ (0,3)(1,2),\\
 &(0,1,2),&(0,1,3),&(0,2,3),\ (1,2,3),\\
 &(0,2,1),&(0,3,1),&(0,3,2),\ (1,3,2).
\end{array}
\tag{2}
\]
We now seek the transitive divisors of $P$, leaving aside the intransitive groups. From each conjugacy class of divisors it is enough to name one representative.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]
\item If a transitive permutation group on four symbols contains a three-cycle, it is either the symmetric group or the alternating group.
\item If it contains two transpositions with a common symbol, it is the symmetric group.
\end{enumerate}

Indeed, from a three-cycle $(0,1,2)$ and transitivity one obtains a three-cycle involving $3$, say $(0,1,3)$; from these one obtains all three-cycles and hence the alternating group. If a transposition is also present, the group is symmetric.

A transitive group different from $P$ and $Q$ that contains a transposition must, if it contains $(0,2)$, also contain $(1,3)$, and hence at least
\[
        1,
\quad (0,2),
\quad (1,3),
\quad (0,2)(1,3).
\tag{3}
\]
For transitivity four more elements must be added. The only possible ones are
\[
        (0,1,2,3),
\quad (0,3,2,1),
\quad (0,3)(1,2),
\quad (0,1)(2,3).
\tag{4}
\]
If one of these is present, all are present, and one obtains the group of degree eight
\[
\begin{array}{rcl}
P_1&=&1,
(0,2),
(1,3),
(0,2)(1,3),
(0,1)(2,3),
(0,3)(1,2),\\[0.2em]
&& (0,1,2,3),
(0,3,2,1).
\end{array}
\tag{5}
\]
There are three conjugate groups of this type. Their intersection is the normal divisor
\[
        Q_1=1,
(0,2)(1,3),
(0,1)(2,3),
(0,3)(1,2).
\tag{6}
\]

If a transitive group different from $P$ and $Q$ contains no transposition, then either it is $Q_1$, or it contains a four-cycle and hence a cyclic group
\[
        P_2=1,
(0,1,2,3),
(0,2)(1,3),
(0,3,2,1).
\tag{7}
\]
The three conjugate groups of type $P_2$ have no common divisor except the identity. Apart from $P$, $Q$, $P_1$, $Q_1$, $P_2$, and the conjugates of $P_1$ and $P_2$, there are no transitive permutation groups on four elements. Among the intransitive groups one should note
\[
        P_3=1,
(0,2),
(1,3),
(0,2)(1,3),
\]
which likewise gives rise to a system of three conjugate groups.




\sect{161}{Solution of biquadratic equations}

The different methods for solving a biquadratic equation differ only in the order in which one uses the several divisors of the group. Let
\[
        \alpha,\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\alpha_3
\]
be the roots of
\[
        f(x)=x^4-a_1x^3+a_2x^2-a_3x+a_4=0.        \tag{1}
\]
Let $D$ be the discriminant and put
\[
 \sqrt D=(\alpha-\alpha_1)(\alpha-\alpha_2)(\alpha-\alpha_3)
(\alpha_1-\alpha_2)(\alpha_1-\alpha_3)(\alpha_2-\alpha_3).        \tag{2}
\]
We also recall the invariants
\[
        A=a_2^2-3a_1a_3+12a_4,                                      \tag{3}
\]
\[
        B=27a_1^2a_4+27a_3^2+2a_2^3-72a_2a_4-9a_1a_2a_3,              \tag{4}
\]
for which
\[
        27D=4A^3-B^2.                                                  \tag{5}
\]

If one first adjoins $\sqrt D$, the group is reduced to the alternating group $Q$. If one then seeks a function belonging to the divisor $Q_1$, the group is further reduced to $Q_1$. Since $Q_1$ has index $3$ in $Q$, such a function depends on a cubic normal equation. Take
\[
        y=(\alpha-\alpha_1)(\alpha_2-\alpha_3).                       \tag{6}
\]
The conjugate values are
\[
        y_1=-(\alpha-\alpha_2)(\alpha_3-\alpha_1),\qquad
        y_2=(\alpha-\alpha_3)(\alpha_1-\alpha_2).                     \tag{7}
\]
They are roots of the cubic partial resolvent
\[
        y^3-Ay+\sqrt D=0.                                              \tag{8}
\]
Its discriminant is $B^2$, and with the sign fixed by Gauss's convention,
\[
        (y-y_1)(y-y_2)(y_1-y_2)=B.                                     \tag{9}
\]
The equation is a normal equation, so $y_1,y_2$ are rational functions of $y$. From
\[
 (y-y_1)(y-y_2)=3y^2-A,
 \qquad
 (y_1-y_2)^2=4A-3y^2
\]
one obtains
\[
        B(y_1-y_2)=-9y^4+15Ay^2-4A^2=6Ay^2+9y\sqrt D-4A^2,
\]
and hence
\[
        y_1+y_2=-y.                                                    \tag{10}
\]
After $y$ is adjoined, the group is reduced to
\[
        Q_1=1,\ (0,2)(1,3),\ (0,1)(2,3),\ (0,3)(1,2).
\]
This group is imprimitive; the equation is then solved by two square roots. Put
\[
\begin{aligned}
        v_1&=(\alpha+\alpha_1-\alpha_2-\alpha_3)^2,\\
        v_2&=(\alpha-\alpha_1+\alpha_2-\alpha_3)^2,\\
        v_3&=(\alpha-\alpha_1-\alpha_2+\alpha_3)^2.
\end{aligned}                                                          \tag{11}
\]
These quantities are fixed by $Q_1$ and are therefore rational in $y$:
\[
\begin{aligned}
        3v_1&=3a_1^2-8a_2-4y_1+4y_2,\\
        3v_2&=3a_1^2-8a_2-4y_2+4y,\\
        3v_3&=3a_1^2-8a_2-4y+4y_1.
\end{aligned}                                                          \tag{12}
\]
The signs of their square roots are tied by
\[
        \sqrt{v_1}\sqrt{v_2}\sqrt{v_3}=a_1^3-4a_1a_2+8a_3.             \tag{13}
\]
With this convention,
\[
\begin{aligned}
        \sqrt{v_1}&=\alpha+\alpha_1-\alpha_2-\alpha_3,\\
        \sqrt{v_2}&=\alpha-\alpha_1+\alpha_2-\alpha_3,\\
        \sqrt{v_3}&=\alpha-\alpha_1-\alpha_2+\alpha_3,
\end{aligned}                                                          \tag{14}
\]
and therefore
\[
\begin{aligned}
4\alpha   &=a_1+\sqrt{v_1}+\sqrt{v_2}+\sqrt{v_3},\\
4\alpha_1 &=a_1+\sqrt{v_1}-\sqrt{v_2}-\sqrt{v_3},\\
4\alpha_2 &=a_1-\sqrt{v_1}+\sqrt{v_2}-\sqrt{v_3},\\
4\alpha_3 &=a_1-\sqrt{v_1}-\sqrt{v_2}+\sqrt{v_3}.
\end{aligned}                                                          \tag{15}
\]
Whatever branches are chosen, provided (13) is preserved, these expressions give the four roots in some order.

Other routes give the same solution. If one adjoins a function belonging to $P_1$ rather than first adjoining $\sqrt D$, one obtains a non-normal cubic resolvent. Taking $y^2$ gives
\[
